   - D) Nawab of Arcot
   - **Answer: A**
   - Explanation: In 1632, the Sultan of Golconda issued the famous 'Golden Farman', allowing the British to trade freely in his kingdom's ports for a fixed annual payment of 500 Pagodas.
Why others are wrong: Shah Jahan was the Mughal Emperor, not the regional ruler of Golconda; the Nizam of Hyderabad state emerged later in the 18th century; the Nawab of Arcot ruled the Carnatic region under Hyderabad.

17. **Which English company official was responsible for the foundation of Fort St. George in Madras in 1639?**
   - A) Job Charnock
   - B) Francis Day
   - C) Gerald Aungier
   - D) Thomas Best
   - **Answer: B**
   - Explanation: Francis Day obtained a lease for a strip of land from the local Naika ruler of Chandragiri in 1639, where the British constructed the fortified factory named Fort St. George.
Why others are wrong: Job Charnock founded Fort William in Calcutta; Gerald Aungier was the architect of early Bombay's growth; Thomas Best was a naval commander at the Battle of Swally.

18. **The Second Carnatic War (1749â€“1754) was triggered primarily by which local political crisis?**
   - A) A dispute over the throne of Bengal between Siraj-ud-daulah and Mir Jafar
   - B) Simultaneous succession disputes in the Nizamship of Hyderabad and Nawabship of the Carnatic
   - C) The invasion of Nadir Shah into northern India
   - D) The annexation of Mysore by the Maratha Confederacy
   - **Answer: B**
   - Explanation: The Second Carnatic War arose from internal proxy conflicts over succession after the deaths of Nizam-ul-Mulk of Hyderabad and Anwar-ud-din of Carnatic, with the British and French backing rival claimants.
Why others are wrong: Bengal disputes led to the Battle of Plassey; Nadir Shah's invasion occurred in 1739; Mysore conflicts led to the Anglo-Mysore Wars later in the century.

19. **Who led the brilliant British defense and siege of Arcot in 1751, turning the tide against the French coalition during the Second Carnatic War?**
   - A) Robert Clive
   - B) Warren Hastings
   - C) Eyre Coote
   - D) Lord Cornwallis
   - **Answer: A**
   - Explanation: Robert Clive, then a young captain, led a diversionary attack capturing Arcot, the capital of the Carnatic, and successfully defended it for 53 days against overwhelming numbers, dealing a blow to French prestige.
Why others are wrong: Warren Hastings was the first Governor-General much later; Eyre Coote won the Battle of Wandiwash; Cornwallis fought in the Third Anglo-Mysore War.

20. **The Cartaz system, a highly restrictive naval licensing system forcing all merchant ships to buy trade passes, was introduced in the Indian Ocean by which power?**
   - A) The British
   - B) The Dutch
   - C) The French
   - D) The Portuguese
   - **Answer: D**
   - Explanation: The Portuguese used their naval dominance to enforce the Cartaz system. Any ship sailing the Indian Ocean had to buy a permit and pay customs duties, failing which they were captured or sunk.
Why others are wrong: The British, Dutch, and French did use trade monopolies, but the specific, aggressive 16th-century naval licensing pass system known as 'Cartaz' was an invention of the Portuguese State.

21. **Which French explorer founded the settlement of Pondicherry in 1674, turning it into the premier French headquarters in India?**
   - A) Caron
   - B) Marcara
   - C) FranÃ§ois Martin
   - D) Dupleix
   - **Answer: C**
   - Explanation: FranÃ§ois Martin obtained a site from the ruler of Valikondapuram and laid the foundations of Pondicherry, transforming a small village into a major commercial port city.
Why others are wrong: Francois Caron established the very first French factory at Surat in 1667; Marcara established a factory at Masulipatnam; Dupleix became governor much later in 1742.

22. **In 1717, which Mughal Emperor issued a royal farman granting the British East India Company duty-free trade rights in Bengal, often called the 'Magna Carta' of the Company?**
   - A) Bahadur Shah I
   - B) Farrukhsiyar
   - C) Muhammad Shah
   - D) Shah Alam II
   - **Answer: B**
   - Explanation: Emperor Farrukhsiyar, after being cured of a painful disease by a British surgeon named William Hamilton, granted a comprehensive farman giving the company immense trade privileges in Bengal, Bombay, and Surat.
Why others are wrong: Bahadur Shah I ruled before 1717; Muhammad Shah was the ruler during Nadir Shah's invasion; Shah Alam II signed the Treaty of Allahabad in 1765 after the Battle of Buxar.

23. **The Danish East India Company established its principal headquarters in India at which place in 1620?**
   - A) Serampore
   - B) Tranquebar
   - C) Calicut
   - D) Chandernagore
   - **Answer: B**
   - Explanation: The Danes arrived in India in 1620 and established their first major factory and settlement at Tranquebar (Tharangambadi) on the Tamil Nadu coast.
Why others are wrong: Serampore in Bengal was their later settlement founded in 1755; Calicut was a Portuguese stronghold; Chandernagore was a prominent French settlement in Bengal.

24. **Which treaty concluded the Third Carnatic War in 1763, restoring Pondicherry to the French but forbidding them from fortifying their settlements?**
   - A) Treaty of Paris
   - B) Treaty of Vienna
   - C) Treaty of London
   - D) Treaty of Utrecht
   - **Answer: A**
   - Explanation: The Treaty of Paris (1763) ended the global Seven Years' War. The French were allowed to keep their factories in India (like Pondicherry and Chandernagore) only as trading centers and were barred from keeping armies or building fortifications.
Why others are wrong: Treaty of Vienna was signed in 1815; Treaty of London and Utrecht related to other European territorial balances not directly ending the Carnatic Wars.

25. **The term 'Interlopers' during the 17th and 18th centuries in India referred to which group?**
   - A) Local Indian middlemen who negotiated between European traders and weavers
   - B) Independent British merchant traders who operated in violation of the East India Company's official monopoly
   - C) French secret agents sent to stir up trouble in Rajput courts
   - D) Portuguese pirates operating out of Chittagong
   - **Answer: B**
   - Explanation: Interlopers were private British merchants who engaged in free trade in Asia, defying the strict legal monopoly granted by the British Crown to the East India Company.
Why others are wrong: Indian middlemen were known as Dubashes or Gomastas; French agents were military advisors, not referred to as interlopers; Portuguese pirates were called Harmads or Feringis.

26. **Why did the Portuguese lose their initial popularity and dominance in India during the 17th century?**
   - A) Their discovery of Brazil diverted their colonization interests and resources westward
   - B) They suffered from internal political instability due to the union of the Portuguese and Spanish crowns (1580â€“1640)
   - C) Their aggressive religious intolerance, forced conversions, and piracy alienated local rulers
   - D) All of the above
   - **Answer: D**
   - Explanation: A combination of factors caused the Portuguese decline: the discovery of Brazil pulled away manpower; the Spanish annexation dragged Portugal into wars with England and the Dutch; and their high-handed religious persecution and piracy destroyed trade alliances.
Why others are wrong: Since all choices A, B, and C are historically correct factors, D is the only comprehensive answer.

27. **Which British officer obtained the site of Calcutta (Sutanuti, Gobindapur, and Kalikata) to establish a fortified settlement in 1690?**
   - A) Gerald Aungier
   - B) Job Charnock
   - C) Charles Eyre
   - D) John Surman
   - **Answer: B**
   - Explanation: Job Charnock, an agent of the East India Company, chose Sutanuti as the site for the company's operations in 1690, which eventually grew into the metropolis of Calcutta centered around Fort William.
Why others are wrong: Gerald Aungier was associated with Bombay; Charles Eyre was the first president of Fort William but didn't secure the initial site; John Surman led the embassy to Farrukhsiyar in 1715.

28. **What was the primary geopolitical outcome of the three Carnatic Wars fought in southern India?**
   - A) The complete elimination of the Mughal Empire's authority over the Deccan
   - B) The total eradication of French commercial presence from the Indian subcontinent
   - C) The elimination of France as a political rival, ensuring British dominance over India
   - D) The establishment of a unified independent Maratha kingdom in the Carnatic
   - **Answer: C**
   - Explanation: The Carnatic Wars settled the colonial rivalry between the two superpowers. France was reduced to an insignificant political entity in India, clearing the path for the British East India Company to dominate the country.
Why others are wrong: The French were not totally eradicated (they kept Pondicherry as a trade port); the Mughal empire's power was already declining independently; the Marathas did not establish a unified kingdom there as a result of these wars.

29. **The United East India Company of the Netherlands (VOC) was governed by a central board of directors known as:**
   - A) The Court of Directors
   - B) The Gentlemen XVII (Heeren XVII)
   - C) The Council of State
   - D) The Board of Control
   - **Answer: B**
   - Explanation: The Dutch East India Company was structured as a federation of chambers managed by a assembly of 17 directors, known historically as the 'Heeren XVII' or Gentlemen XVII.
Why others are wrong: The Court of Directors and Board of Control were governing bodies of the British East India Company under the Regulating and Pitt's India Acts.

30. **Who was the ruler of Calicut (Kozhikode) who warmly received Vasco da Gama when he landed on the shores of India in 1498?**
   - A) The Zamorin (Samudiri)
   - B) Krishna Deva Raya
   - C) Yusuf Adil Shah
   - D) Martanda Varma
   - **Answer: A**
   - Explanation: When Vasco da Gama arrived at Calicut on May 20, 1498, he was received by the local Hindu ruler who held the hereditary title of the Zamorin.
Why others are wrong: Krishna Deva Raya was the emperor of Vijayanagara; Yusuf Adil Shah was the Sultan of Bijapur from whom Goa was captured; Martanda Varma was the king of Travancore in the 18th century.

## Modern - British Expansion (Plassey, Buxar, Anglo-Mysore, Anglo-Maratha, Anglo-Sikh Wars) (Modern)

1.  Topic Introduction
    The given questions focus on Modern Indian History, specifically covering the British Expansion in India through key military conflicts and treaties. This encompasses the transition of the British East India Company from a mere trading entity into the supreme political power in India by systematically subduing major regional powers including the Nawabs of Bengal, the Mysore Kingdom, the Maratha Confederacy, and the Sikh Empire. This foundational era is a high-yield, mandatory core segment of the General Awareness (History) syllabus for the SSC CGL exam.

2.  Core Concepts Explained Simply
    
      - Mercantilism to Imperialism: The British East India Company leveraged local political instability, betrayals, and advanced military tactics to transform their trading privileges into absolute territorial sovereignty.
      - Battle of Plassey (1757) & Buxar (1764): The twin battles that laid the actual foundation of British rule in India. Plassey gave them a political foothold in Bengal through treachery, while Buxar established their military superiority over a combined Indian coalition and yielded administrative revenue rights.
      - Subsidiary Alliance System: A clever diplomatic-military framework introduced by Lord Wellesley where Indian rulers paid a subsidy to maintain a British army in their territory, effectively surrendering their foreign relations and independence without a direct fight.
      - Doctrine of Lapse: A controversial annexation policy heavily utilized by Lord Dalhousie where any princely state under the direct or indirect control of the East India Company would automatically be annexed if the ruler died without a natural male heir.

3.  Important Facts and Dates
    
      - 1757: Battle of Plassey (Robert Clive defeats Nawab Siraj-ud-daulah due to Mir Jafar's betrayal).
      - 1764: Battle of Buxar (Hector Munro defeats the allied forces of Mir Qasim, Shuja-ud-daulah, and Shah Alam II).
      - 1765: Treaty of Allahabad (Grants Diwani rights of Bengal, Bihar, and Odisha to the British East India Company).
      - 1782: Treaty of Salbai (Concludes the First Anglo-Maratha War, securing a 20-year peace).
      - 1799: Fourth Anglo-Mysore War (Tipu Sultan dies defending Seringapatam; Mysore falls to a Subsidiary Alliance).
      - 1849: Annexation of Punjab (Lord Dalhousie formally annexes the Sikh Empire after the Second Anglo-Sikh War).

4.  Related Concepts Frequently Asked in SSC Exams
    
      - Regulatory and Administrative Acts: Following territorial expansions, the British Parliament regulated the Company via the Regulating Act of 1773 and subsequent Charter Acts, which are heavily tested.
      - Land Revenue Systems: Post-expansion administrative changes like the Permanent Settlement in Bengal (Cornwallis, 1793), Ryotwari System in Madras/Bombay (Munro), and Mahalwari System in the North-West.
      - Economic Drain Theory: Conceptualized by Dadabhai Naoroji in his book "Poverty and Un-British Rule in India," explaining how the wealth generated from these expansions was systematically siphoned back to Britain.

5.  Differences Between Similar Terms
    
      - Treaty of Surat (1775) vs. Treaty of Bassein (1802): The Treaty of Surat was signed by Raghunath Rao and triggered the First Anglo-Maratha War, whereas the Treaty of Bassein was signed by Peshwa Baji Rao II and directly triggered the Second Anglo-Maratha War.
      - Diwani Rights vs. Nizamat Rights: Diwani rights represented the authority to collect revenues and manage civil justice (acquired by the British in 1765), whereas Nizamat rights referred to military defense, police, and criminal administration, which remained with the puppet Nawab under the 'Dual Government' system.
      - First Anglo-Sikh War vs. Second Anglo-Sikh War: The First War (1845-1846) resulted in a peace treaty (Treaty of Lahore) keeping a sovereign but weakened Sikh state, while the Second War (1848-1849) resulted in absolute British military victory and the immediate total annexation of the Punjab region.

6.  Examples
    
      - Use of Mysorean Rockets: The military engagements under Hyder Ali and Tipu Sultan illustrate the early advanced deployment of iron-cased rocket artillery, which temporarily destabilized traditional British infantry squares.
      - Treachery as a Tactical Tool: The Battle of Plassey serves as the ultimate example of political conspiracy where British victory was guaranteed before the fight even began through secret agreements with the Nawab's commander, Mir Jafar.
      - Creation of Buffer States: Following the Battle of Buxar, the British did not immediately annex Awadh; instead, they signed the Treaty of Allahabad to turn Awadh into a dependent buffer state to protect Bengal from potential Maratha invasions.

7.  Previous-Year Exam Perspective
    
      - SSC CGL exams consistently focus on matching treaties with their corresponding wars (e.g., Treaty of Mangalore with the Second Anglo-Mysore War, or Treaty of Seringapatam with the Third Anglo-Mysore War). Questions frequently target the exact names of Governor-Generals responsible for major annexations or policy implementations (such as Wellesley for Subsidiary Alliances and Dalhousie for the Doctrine of Lapse). Additionally, specific operational details like the nicknames of battles (e.g., Battle of Gujrat as the 'Battle of the Guns') and the identities of traitorous figures are regular targets for multiple-choice questions.

8.  Quick Revision Points and Mnemonics
    
      - Buxar Commander: Munro at Buxar (Munro sounds like Arrow, shot at Buxar).
      - Plassey Commander: Clive at Plassey (Clive captured Calcutta and triumphed at Plassey).
      - Mysore Treaties: M-M-S (Madras - 1st War, Mangalore - 2nd War, Seringapatam - 3rd War).
      - Battle of the Guns: Gujrat = Guns (Both start with G).
      - Subsidiary Alliance Pioneer: Wellesley = Wellesley's Welfare Alliance (An alliance claiming to offer protection but taking over independence).
      - Maratha Ending Battle: Ashti = Finished the Marathas (Ashti sounds like 'Ast', meaning sunset or end of the Empire in 1818).

### Quick Practice (30 MCQs)

1. **Who was the Nawab of Bengal during the Battle of Plassey in 1757?**
   - A) Mir Jafar
   - B) Siraj-ud-daulah
   - C) Mir Qasim
   - D) Alivardi Khan
   - **Answer: B**
   - Explanation: Siraj-ud-daulah was the Nawab of Bengal who confronted the British East India Company led by Robert Clive at the Battle of Plassey in 1757. Why others are wrong: Mir Jafar was the commander who betrayed Siraj-ud-daulah and became a puppet Nawab after the battle; Mir Qasim succeeded Mir Jafar later and fought the Battle of Buxar; Alivardi Khan was the grandfather of Siraj-ud-daulah and the previous Nawab who died in 1756.

2. **Which treaty brought an end to the First Anglo-Maratha War in 1782, establishing peace between the two sides for twenty years?**
   - A) Treaty of Salbai
   - B) Treaty of Purandar
   - C) Treaty of Surat
   - D) Treaty of Bassein
   - **Answer: A**
   - Explanation: The Treaty of Salbai was signed in May 1782 by representatives of the Maratha Empire and the British East India Company, settling the First Anglo-Maratha War by restoring the status quo. Why others are wrong: Treaty of Surat initiated the war in 1775; Treaty of Purandar was signed in 1776 but failed to establish permanent peace; Treaty of Bassein was signed in 1802 by Baji Rao II, which actually triggered the Second Anglo-Maratha War.

3. **In which battle did the combined forces of Mir Qasim, Shuja-ud-daulah, and Shah Alam II fight against the British East India Company in 1764?**
   - A) Battle of Plassey
   - B) Battle of Buxar
   - C) Battle of Wandiwash
   - D) Battle of Chillianwala
   - **Answer: B**
   - Explanation: The Battle of Buxar was fought on October 22, 1764, between the British forces led by Hector Munro and the combined allied army of Mir Qasim (Nawab of Bengal), Shuja-ud-daulah (Nawab of Awadh), and Mughal Emperor Shah Alam II. Why others are wrong: Battle of Plassey took place in 1757 solely against Siraj-ud-daulah; Battle of Wandiwash was fought in 1760 between the British and French; Battle of Chillianwala was fought during the Second Anglo-Sikh War in 1849.

4. **Who was the ruler of Mysore during the First and Second Anglo-Mysore Wars before passing away in 1782?**
   - A) Tipu Sultan
   - B) Hyder Ali
   - C) Chikka Devaraja
   - D) Krishna Raja Wadiyar III
   - **Answer: B**
   - Explanation: Hyder Ali rose to power in Mysore and fiercely fought the British in the First and Second Anglo-Mysore Wars until his death from cancer during the Second War in 1782. Why others are wrong: Tipu Sultan was his son who took command after 1782; Chikka Devaraja was an earlier 17th-century ruler; Krishna Raja Wadiyar III was placed on the throne as a child by the British after Tipu's defeat in 1799.

5. **The Treaty of Seringapatam in 1792 concluded which of the following conflicts?**
   - A) Second Anglo-Mysore War
   - B) Third Anglo-Mysore War
   - C) Fourth Anglo-Mysore War
   - D) First Anglo-Maratha War
   - **Answer: B**
   - Explanation: The Third Anglo-Mysore War ended with the Treaty of Seringapatam in 1792, forcing Tipu Sultan to surrender half of his kingdom to the British and their allies. Why others are wrong: The Second Anglo-Mysore War ended with the Treaty of Mangalore in 1784; the Fourth Anglo-Mysore War ended with Tipu's death in 1799 without a treaty; the First Anglo-Maratha War ended with the Treaty of Salbai.

6. **Which British Governor-General introduced the Subsidiary Alliance system, which was heavily utilized to subjugate Hyderabad, Mysore, and the Marathas?**
   - A) Lord Warren Hastings
   - B) Lord Cornwallis
   - C) Lord Richard Wellesley
   - D) Lord Dalhousie
   - **Answer: C**
   - Explanation: Lord Wellesley, Governor-General from 1798 to 1805, extensively utilized the Subsidiary Alliance system to bring Indian princely states under British control by offering protection in exchange for military subsidies and control over foreign relations. Why others are wrong: Warren Hastings expanded territory through direct wars; Cornwallis introduced the Permanent Settlement in Bengal; Dalhousie introduced the Doctrine of Lapse much later.

7. **During which war did Tipu Sultan lose his life while defending his capital city?**
   - A) Second Anglo-Mysore War
   - B) Third Anglo-Mysore War
   - C) Fourth Anglo-Mysore War
   - D) First Anglo-Sikh War
   - **Answer: C**
   - Explanation: Tipu Sultan was killed on May 4, 1799, during the Fourth Anglo-Mysore War while defending his fort city of Seringapatam against the British forces led by General George Harris. Why others are wrong: The Second War ended with a status quo treaty; the Third War resulted in Tipu losing half his territory but surviving; the First Anglo-Sikh War took place in the Punjab region decades later.

8. **The Treaty of Deogaon (1803) was signed by which Maratha chief during the Second Anglo-Maratha War?**
   - A) Bhonsle
   - B) Scindia
   - C) Holkar
   - D) Peshwa Baji Rao II
   - **Answer: A**
   - Explanation: Following defeats by the British forces under Arthur Wellesley, the Maratha ruler Bhonsle of Berar signed the Treaty of Deogaon in December 1803, surrendering Cuttack and Balasore. Why others are wrong: Scindia signed the Treaty of Surji-Anjangaon; Holkar continued fighting until signing the Treaty of Rajghat in 1805; Peshwa Baji Rao II signed the Treaty of Bassein in 1802 before the war broke out.

9. **Which battle marked the definitive end of the Maratha Empire's independence in 1818 during the Third Anglo-Maratha War?**
   - A) Battle of Assaye
   - B) Battle of Kirkee
   - C) Battle of Ashti
   - D) Battle of Laswari
   - **Answer: C**
   - Explanation: The defeat of Peshwa Baji Rao II's forces at the Battle of Ashti in February 1818 led to his eventual surrender, resulting in the abolition of the Peshwaship and total British dominance over the Maratha territories. Why others are wrong: Battle of Assaye and Battle of Laswari took place during the Second Anglo-Maratha War in 1803; Battle of Kirkee occurred earlier in the Third Anglo-Maratha War (1817) but did not mark the final surrender.

10. **The Treaty of Lahore (1846) was signed at the conclusion of which military campaign?**
   - A) First Anglo-Maratha War
   - B) Second Anglo-Maratha War
   - C) First Anglo-Sikh War
   - D) Second Anglo-Sikh War
   - **Answer: C**
   - Explanation: The First Anglo-Sikh War (1845-1846) ended with the Treaty of Lahore, which forced the Sikh Empire to cede the Jalandhar Doab and pay a massive war indemnity. Why others are wrong: The Maratha wars ended with the treaties of Salbai, Surji-Anjangaon, and Mandasor; the Second Anglo-Sikh War resulted in total annexation rather than a treaty retaining the sovereign Sikh State.

11. **Who was the sovereign ruler of the Sikh Empire whose death in 1839 led to political instability and eventual conflict with the British?**
   - A) Maharaja Ranjit Singh
   - B) Maharaja Kharak Singh
   - C) Maharaja Duleep Singh
   - D) Maharaja Sher Singh
   - **Answer: A**
   - Explanation: Maharaja Ranjit Singh built a powerful, modernized Sikh state and maintained a non-aggression pact with the British. His death in 1839 created a leadership vacuum that triggered the Anglo-Sikh wars. Why others are wrong: Kharak Singh and Sher Singh were short-lived successors; Duleep Singh was the last Maharaja who was a minor when the wars ended in total annexation.

12. **Which British Governor-General formally annexed the Punjab to the British Empire in 1849?**
   - A) Lord Ellenborough
   - B) Lord Hardinge
   - C) Lord Dalhousie
   - D) Lord Canning
   - **Answer: C**
   - Explanation: Following the decisive British victory in the Second Anglo-Sikh War, Governor-General Lord Dalhousie passed the proclamation of annexation on March 29, 1849, absorbing Punjab into British India. Why others are wrong: Ellenborough annexed Sindh; Hardinge oversaw the First Anglo-Sikh War; Canning succeeded Dalhousie and dealt with the 1857 revolt.

13. **What was the primary immediate outcome of the Treaty of Allahabad signed in 1765?**
   - A) The execution of Mir Qasim
   - B) The granting of Diwani rights of Bengal, Bihar, and Odisha to the British East India Company
   - C) The annexation of Awadh
   - D) The termination of French presence in India
   - **Answer: B**
   - Explanation: Concluding the Battle of Buxar, the Treaty of Allahabad was signed between Robert Clive and Mughal Emperor Shah Alam II, granting the Company the Diwani rights (the right to collect revenues) for Bengal, Bihar, and Odisha. Why others are wrong: Mir Qasim escaped and died in poverty; Awadh was not annexed but turned into a buffer state; French presence had already been largely curbed by the Treaty of Paris in 1763.

14. **In which battle of the Second Anglo-Sikh War did the British suffer exceptionally heavy casualties, leading to widespread shock in Britain?**
   - A) Battle of Ramnagar
   - B) Battle of Chillianwala
   - C) Battle of Gujrat
   - D) Battle of Aliwal
   - **Answer: B**
   - Explanation: Fought on January 13, 1849, the Battle of Chillianwala saw ferocious resistance from the Sikh forces under Sher Singh Attariwalla, inflicting severe losses on the British army led by Lord Gough. Why others are wrong: Ramnagar was an early skirmish; Gujrat was the final, decisive British victory; Aliwal was fought during the First Anglo-Sikh War.

15. **The Battle of Gujrat (1849) is famously known as the 'Battle of the Guns' due to the extensive use of what?**
   - A) Matchlock muskets
   - B) Artillery
   - C) Rocket brigades
   - D) Cavalry charges
   - **Answer: B**
   - Explanation: The Battle of Gujrat was the final battle of the Second Anglo-Sikh War where the British deployed overwhelming artillery fire to break the Sikh lines, earning it the nickname 'Battle of the Guns'. Why others are wrong: Matchlocks were outdated; rockets were famously used by Mysore, not in this battle; cavalry was used but was not the defining characteristic of this engagement.

16. **Who led the British East India Company's forces at the Battle of Plassey?**
   - A) Hector Munro
   - B) Eyre Coote
   - C) Robert Clive
   - D) Warren Hastings
   - **Answer: C**
   - Explanation: Robert Clive led the Company's military forces at Plassey in 1757, conspiring with local disgruntled nobles to defeat Nawab Siraj-ud-daulah. Why others are wrong: Hector Munro led the forces at Buxar; Eyre Coote won the Battle of Wandiwash against the French; Warren Hastings was the later civilian Governor-General.

17. **The Treaty of Mangalore (1784) brought a temporary halt to which war?**
   - A) First Anglo-Mysore War
   - B) Second Anglo-Mysore War
   - C) Third Anglo-Mysore War
   - D) First Anglo-Maratha War
   - **Answer: B**
   - Explanation: Signed between Tipu Sultan and the British East India Company, the Treaty of Mangalore ended the Second Anglo-Mysore War on the basis of mutual restitution of conquests. Why others are wrong: The First War ended with the Treaty of Madras; the Third ended with the Treaty of Seringapatam; the First Anglo-Maratha War ended with the Treaty of Salbai.

18. **What triggered the First Anglo-Maratha War?**
   - A) The murder of Narayan Rao and Raghunath Rao's subsequent treaty with the British
   - B) The assassination of Nana Phadnavis
   - C) The annexation of Berar by the British
   - D) Baji Rao II signing the Treaty of Bassein
   - **Answer: A**
   - Explanation: Following the murder of Peshwa Narayan Rao, his uncle Raghunath Rao sought British support to claim the title and signed the Treaty of Surat (1775), which directly caused the First Anglo-Maratha War. Why others are wrong: Nana Phadnavis died much later in 1800; Berar was not annexed at this stage; the Treaty of Bassein triggered the Second War.

19. **Which battle during the First Anglo-Sikh War saw the British successfully cross the Sutlej River after a hard-fought victory?**
   - A) Battle of Mudki
   - B) Battle of Ferozeshah
   - C) Battle of Sobraon
   - D) Battle of Aliwal
   - **Answer: C**
   - Explanation: The Battle of Sobraon (February 10, 1846) was the decisive battle of the First Anglo-Sikh War, leading to the collapse of the Sikh defense and allowing British forces to march on Lahore. Why others are wrong: Mudki and Ferozeshah were early, costly engagements; Aliwal was a brilliant tactical victory by Harry Smith but not the final crossing battle.

20. **The system of 'Dual Government' in Bengal, established after the Battle of Buxar, was abolished by whom?**
   - A) Lord Cornwallis
   - B) Warren Hastings
   - C) Lord Wellesley
   - D) Lord William Bentinck
   - **Answer: B**
   - Explanation: Warren Hastings abolished the Dual Government system in 1772, bringing Bengal under the direct administrative control of the British East India Company. Why others are wrong: Cornwallis introduced judicial reforms later; Wellesley focused on alliances; Bentinck is famous for social reforms in the 1830s.

21. **Which territory did the British acquire from the Sikhs through the Treaty of Amritsar (1846), which they subsequently sold to Gulab Singh?**
   - A) Jalandhar Doab
   - B) Kashmir
   - C) Ladakh
   - D) Peshawar
   - **Answer: B**
   - Explanation: Under the Treaty of Amritsar in March 1846, the British sold the valley of Kashmir to Raja Gulab Singh of Jammu for 75 lakh Nanakshahi rupees, establishing the princely state of Jammu and Kashmir. Why others are wrong: Jalandhar Doab was directly annexed by the British; Ladakh was already under Dogra control; Peshawar remained with the Sikh state until 1849.

22. **During the Anglo-Mysore wars, what innovative weapon did Hyder Ali and Tipu Sultan use effectively against British infantry formations?**
   - A) Iron-cased rockets
   - B) Flintlock rifles
   - C) Breech-loading cannons
   - D) Heavy armored cavalry
   - **Answer: A**
   - Explanation: Mysore was pioneer in the military use of iron-cased Mysorean rockets, which caused massive disruption and terror among British troops due to their long range and explosive power. Why others are wrong: Flintlocks were standard European tech; breech-loading artillery wasn't mature yet; heavy armored cavalry was an outdated medieval tactic.

23. **Who was the British commander who defeated Scindia's forces at the Battle of Assaye in 1803?**
   - A) Arthur Wellesley
   - B) Lord Lake
   - C) Lord Gough
   - D) Charles Napier
   - **Answer: A**
   - Explanation: Major General Arthur Wellesley (later the Duke of Wellington) achieved one of his finest tactical victories against the combined Maratha forces of Scindia and Bhonsle at Assaye in September 1803. Why others are wrong: Lord Lake commanded the northern British theater during the same war; Lord Gough fought the Sikhs; Charles Napier conquered Sindh.

24. **What was the primary reason for the Second Anglo-Sikh War (1848-1849)?**
   - A) The Sikh invasion of British Delhi
   - B) The rebellion of Diwan Mulraj of Multan
   - C) The refusal of Rani Jindan to leave Punjab
   - D) The assassination of Maharaja Ranjit Singh
   - **Answer: B**
   - Explanation: The revolt of Diwan Mulraj, the governor of Multan, who killed two British officers, triggered a wider uprising among Sikh veterans that escalated into the Second Anglo-Sikh War. Why others are wrong: The Sikhs never invaded Delhi; Rani Jindan was already exiled or neutralized; Ranjit Singh died naturally in 1839, long before this war.

25. **Which document gave the British East India Company the legal right to collect customs-free trade throughout Bengal prior to the Battle of Plassey?**
   - A) The Farman of Emperor Farrukhsiyar
   - B) The Treaty of Alinagar
   - C) The Regulating Act of 1773
   - D) The Charter Act of 1813
   - **Answer: A**
   - Explanation: In 1717, Mughal Emperor Farrukhsiyar issued a royal decree (Farman) granting the Company the right to trade duty-free in Bengal in exchange for a fixed annual payment, which later became a major source of friction with local Nawabs. Why others are wrong: Treaty of Alinagar was signed right before Plassey; Regulating Act and Charter Act were British Parliamentary laws passed much later.

26. **In 1780, Hyder Ali inflicted a crushing defeat on a British force commanded by Colonel William Baillie at which location?**
   - A) Battle of Pollilur
   - B) Battle of Porto Novo
   - C) Battle of Sholinghur
   - D) Battle of Seringapatam
   - **Answer: A**
   - Explanation: The Battle of Pollilur (1780) saw Hyder Ali and Tipu Sultan utterly destroy Baillie's detachment, marking one of the worst disasters suffered by the British in India. Why others are wrong: Porto Novo and Sholinghur were subsequent British victories led by Eyre Coote; Seringapatam was the site of the final siege in 1799.

27. **Which Maratha leader formed the 'Maratha Confederacy' or alliance that fought the British during the Second Anglo-Maratha War?**
   - A) Nana Phadnavis
   - B) Mahadji Scindia
   - C) Yashwantrao Holkar
   - D) Raghunath Rao
   - **Answer: C**
   - Explanation: Yashwantrao Holkar, along with Scindia and Bhonsle, formed the core resistances, though they often fought independently due to mutual distrust. Holkar successfully resisted Lord Lake's forces at Bharatpur. Why others are wrong: Nana Phadnavis and Mahadji Scindia had passed away before the war began; Raghunath Rao was a pro-British conspirator.

28. **The Treaty of Mandasor (1818) ended the hostilities between the British and which Maratha faction?**
   - A) The Peshwas
   - B) The Holkars
   - C) The Scindias
   - D) The Bhonsles
   - **Answer: B**
   - Explanation: Following their defeat at the Battle of Mahidpur during the Third Anglo-Maratha War, the Holkar dynasty signed the Treaty of Mandasor, becoming a subsidiary state under the British. Why others are wrong: The Peshwa's territory was annexed directly; Scindia and Bhonsle signed separate treaties during the second and third wars.

29. **What name was given to the fortified factory built by the British East India Company in Calcutta, the fortification of which angered Nawab Siraj-ud-daulah?**
   - A) Fort St. George
   - B) Fort William
   - C) Fort St. David
   - D) Fort Aguada
   - **Answer: B**
   - Explanation: Fort William was established by the British in Calcutta. The unauthorized expansion of its fortifications by the British without the Nawab's permission led Siraj-ud-daulah to capture it in 1756. Why others are wrong: Fort St. George was located in Madras; Fort St. David was near Cuddalore; Fort Aguada was a Portuguese fort in Goa.

30. **Which battle during the First Anglo-Sikh War is notorious for the betrayal of the Sikh commanders Lal Singh and Tej Singh, who abandoned their troops?**
   - A) Battle of Ferozeshah
   - B) Battle of Mudki
   - C) Battle of Gujrat
   - D) Battle of Chillianwala
   - **Answer: A**
   - Explanation: During the Battle of Ferozeshah (1845), the Sikh army had the British forces in a precarious position, but the treachery of commanders Lal Singh and Tej Singh, who refused to attack and ordered a retreat, handed the victory to the British. Why others are wrong: Mudki was the opening clash; Gujrat and Chillianwala were fought during the Second Anglo-Sikh War.

## Modern - Freedom Movement (Revolt of 1857, Causes, Leaders, Consequences) (Modern)

1.  Topic Introduction
    The provided multiple-choice questions focus on Modern Indian History, specifically covering the Revolt of 1857 (often termed the Sepoy Mutiny or the First War of Independence), its causes, key regional leaders, administrative consequences, and the subsequent transfer of power. This pivotal watershed event represents the first major organized challenge to the British East India Company's rule and forms a highly significant, high-yield segment of the General Awareness (History) syllabus for the SSC CGL exam.

2.  Core Concepts Explained Simply  - Economic & Political Grievances: Decades of aggressive expansionist policies like the Doctrine of Lapse and the Subsidiary Alliance, coupled with severe land taxation policies and the annexation of territories like Awadh under the pretext of misgovernance, alienated regional rulers and the peasantry alike.
  - Socio-Religious Trigger: The introduction of greased cartridges for the new Enfield rifle (rumored to contain cow and pig fat) offended the religious sentiments of both Hindu and Muslim sepoys, serving as the immediate catalyst for the mutiny.
  - Military Discontent: Indian sepoys faced systemic discrimination in promotions, lower pay scales compared to British counterparts, and controversial policies like the General Service Enlistment Act of 1856 which mandated overseas service (violating traditional taboos).
  - Administrative Transformation: The failure and suppression of the uprising led to the liquidation of the East India Company. The Government of India Act 1858 shifted governance directly to the British Crown, establishing the office of the Secretary of State for India and altering the Governor-General's title to Viceroy.3.  Important Facts and Dates  - Outbreak Date: The formal collective mutiny began on May 10, 1857, at Meerut, following Mangal Pandey's individual act of defiance on March 29, 1857, at Barrackpore.
  - Leaders & Centers: Bahadur Shah Zafar and Bakht Khan (Delhi), Nana Sahib and Tantia Tope (Kanpur), Begum Hazrat Mahal (Lucknow), Rani Lakshmibai (Jhansi), Kunwar Singh (Bihar/Jagdishpur), and Maulvi Ahmadullah Shah (Faizabad).
  - Crown Takeover: Queen Victoriaâ€™s Proclamation was officially read out by Lord Canning at the Allahabad Durbar on November 1, 1858.4.  Related Concepts Frequently Asked in SSC Exams  - British Commanders: SSC frequently tests the names of British officials who suppressed the rebellion at specific sites (e.g., John Nicholson in Delhi, Colin Campbell in Kanpur/Lucknow, and Hugh Rose in Jhansi).
  - Intellectual/Middle Class Stance: The modern educated Indian middle class and elite remained largely aloof or loyal to the British, viewing the rebellion as a reactionary movement rather than a path to modern progress.
  - Army Restructuring: The Peel Commission recommended a total overhaul of the military structure, drastically reducing the proportion of sepoys from Awadh and Bihar while aggressively recruiting from regions deemed "Martial Races," such as Punjab and Nepal (Gurkhas).5.  Differences Between Similar Terms  - Doctrine of Lapse vs. Subsidiary Alliance: The Subsidiary Alliance (introduced by Wellesley) forced Indian rulers to maintain British troops at their own expense, whereas the Doctrine of Lapse (introduced by Dalhousie) allowed the Company to directly annex any princely state where the ruler died without a natural male heir.
  - Governor-General vs. Viceroy: The Governor-General was the chief executive representing the East India Company; post-1858, the same official assumed the title of Viceroy when acting as the direct representative of the British Monarch.
  - Board of Control vs. Council of India: The Board of Control was part of the Company's dual government system established by Pitt's India Act; it was completely abolished in 1858 and replaced by the 15-member Council of India advising the Secretary of State.6.  Examples  - Social Legislation Resistance: The Lex Loci Act of 1850 (allowing Christian converts to inherit ancestral property) and the Hindu Widows' Remarriage Act of 1856 were viewed by orthodox sections as direct colonial interference in indigenous socio-religious traditions.
  - Guerrilla Strategy: Tantia Tope's operations in central India after the fall of Kanpur and Jhansi highlight the effective deployment of localized hit-and-run guerrilla warfare against superior British military logistics.7.  Previous-Year Exam Perspective  - SSC CGL exams regularly target exact match-the-following patterns pairing historical leaders with their respective rebellion centers (e.g., matching Kunwar Singh to Bihar or Begum Hazrat Mahal to Lucknow). Questions also focus heavily on legislative acts (like the Government of India Act 1858), British Prime Ministers during the crisis (Lord Palmerston), and specific quotes or historiographical perspectives, such as V.D. Savarkar's categorization of the event as the "First War of Indian Independence."8.  Quick Revision Points and Mnemonics  - Immediate Trigger: Enfield Rifles = Greased Cartridges (Cow & Pig fat).
  - Leadership Mnemonic: K-K (Kunwar in Bihar/Jagdishpur near Arrah), L-L (Lucknow led by Lady/Begum Hazrat Mahal), K-N (Kanpur led by Nana Sahib).
  - Suppressor Match: Jhansi = Hugh Rose (Rose fought the Queen of Jhansi); Delhi = Nicholson (Nicholson died capturing Delhi).
  - Political Shift: 1858 Act = End of EIC Rule + Entry of Viceroy (First Viceroy: Lord Canning).

### Quick Practice (30 MCQs)

1. **Who was the Mughal Emperor of India during the Revolt of 1857?**
   - A) Shah Alam II
   - B) Akbar Shah II
   - C) Bahadur Shah Zafar
   - D) Bahadur Shah I
   - **Answer: C**
   - Explanation: Bahadur Shah Zafar was the last Mughal emperor and was declared the symbolic leader of the Revolt of 1857 by the sepoys. Why others are wrong: Shah Alam II ruled during the Battle of Buxar; Akbar Shah II was Zafar's predecessor; Bahadur Shah I ruled in the early 18th century.

2. **Which immediate cause triggered the sepoy mutiny at Meerut in May 1857?**
   - A) The annexation of Awadh
   - B) The introduction of the greased cartridges for the Enfield rifle
   - C) The General Service Enlistment Act
   - D) Heavy taxation on land
   - **Answer: B**
   - Explanation: The new Enfield rifle required soldiers to bite off the top of cartridges, which were rumored to be greased with cow and pig fat, offending both Hindu and Muslim religious sentiments. Why others are wrong: The annexation of Awadh, General Service Enlistment Act, and heavy taxation were deep-rooted long-term causes, not the immediate trigger.

3. **Who led the forces of the Revolt of 1857 in Jhansi?**
   - A) Nana Sahib
   - B) Begum Hazrat Mahal
   - C) Rani Lakshmibai
   - D) Kunwar Singh
   - **Answer: C**
   - Explanation: Rani Lakshmibai, the queen of Jhansi, led the rebellion after her adopted son was denied the right to succeed the throne under the Doctrine of Lapse. Why others are wrong: Nana Sahib led at Kanpur; Begum Hazrat Mahal led at Lucknow; Kunwar Singh led in Bihar.

4. **Under which Governor-General was the controversial 'Doctrine of Lapse' introduced?**
   - A) Lord Canning
   - B) Lord Dalhousie
   - C) Lord Wellesley
   - D) Lord William Bentinck
   - **Answer: B**
   - Explanation: Lord Dalhousie introduced the Doctrine of Lapse, by which any princely state under the direct or indirect control of the East India Company where the ruler did not have a legal male heir would be annexed. Why others are wrong: Lord Canning was the Governor-General during the revolt; Lord Wellesley introduced the Subsidiary Alliance; Lord William Bentinck is known for social reforms like abolishing Sati.

5. **Who was the prominent leader of the 1857 uprising in Bihar?**
   - A) Khan Bahadur
   - B) Kunwar Singh
   - C) Tantia Tope
   - D) Bakht Khan
   - **Answer: B**
   - Explanation: Kunwar Singh, a local zamindar of Jagdishpur near Arrah, led the armed forces during the revolt in Bihar despite his advanced age. Why others are wrong: Khan Bahadur led at Bareilly; Tantia Tope was active in Kanpur and Jhansi; Bakht Khan was the military commander in Delhi.

6. **What was the primary political cause for the resentment of the ruler of Awadh before 1857?**
   - A) Annexation on the grounds of alleged misgovernance
   - B) Doctrine of Lapse application
   - C) Failure to pay the Subsidiary Alliance subsidy
   - D) Abolition of the title of Nawab
   - **Answer: A**
   - Explanation: Lord Dalhousie annexed Awadh in 1856 on the pretext of maladministration, which deeply angered the local nobility and sepoys from the region. Why others are wrong: The Doctrine of Lapse was not applied to Awadh as Wajid Ali Shah had heirs; failure of subsidy was not the reason given; the title was not formally abolished beforehand.

7. **Who served as the loyal commander-in-chief and brilliant guerrilla strategist for Nana Sahib at Kanpur?**
   - A) Tantia Tope
   - B) Azimullah Khan
   - C) Bakht Khan
   - D) Maulvi Ahmadullah
   - **Answer: A**
   - Explanation: Tantia Tope was a close associate and general of Nana Sahib who later joined hands with Rani Lakshmibai to carry out guerrilla warfare against the British. Why others are wrong: Azimullah Khan was Nana Sahib's political advisor; Bakht Khan led the troops at Delhi; Maulvi Ahmadullah led the rebellion in Faizabad.

8. **The General Service Enlistment Act of 1856 caused great resentment among Bengal sepoys because it mandated what?**
   - A) Compulsory deployment overseas crossing the dark waters (Kala Pani)
   - B) A major reduction in monthly salaries
   - C) The mandatory wearing of leather boots
   - D) A ban on keeping religious marks on the forehead
   - **Answer: A**
   - Explanation: The Act required sepoys to serve wherever the Company required, including overseas, which violated the upper-caste Hindu belief that crossing the ocean led to loss of caste. Why others are wrong: The Act did not directly target salaries, boots, or facial religious marks, though those were grievances addressed by other military rules.

9. **Which British official was responsible for suppressing the rebellion in Delhi and capturing Bahadur Shah Zafar?**
   - A) Sir Colin Campbell
   - B) John Nicholson
   - C) General Havelock
   - D) Sir Hugh Rose
   - **Answer: B**
   - Explanation: Brigadier-General John Nicholson led the fierce assault to recapture Delhi, though he was mortally wounded during the fighting. Why others are wrong: Sir Colin Campbell suppressed the revolt in Kanpur and Lucknow; General Havelock fought at Kanpur; Sir Hugh Rose defeated the forces at Jhansi.

10. **Where did the first open mutiny of sepoys occur before they marched toward Delhi?**
   - A) Barrackpore
   - B) Meerut
   - C) Ambala
   - D) Lucknow
   - **Answer: B**
   - Explanation: While Mangal Pandey revolted individually at Barrackpore in March, the formal collective mutiny began at Meerut on May 10, 1857, from where the sepoys marched to Delhi. Why others are wrong: Barrackpore saw an isolated act of defiance; Ambala did not witness the first outbreak; Lucknow rose in revolt later in May.

11. **Who led the revolt in Lucknow, representing the displaced court of Awadh?**
   - A) Zeenat Mahal
   - B) Begum Hazrat Mahal
   - C) Hazrat Shah
   - D) Birjis Qadr
   - **Answer: B**
   - Explanation: Begum Hazrat Mahal, the wife of the deposed Nawab Wajid Ali Shah, took charge of the rebellion in Lucknow on behalf of her minor son Birjis Qadr. Why others are wrong: Zeenat Mahal was the wife of Bahadur Shah Zafar in Delhi; Hazrat Shah is an incorrect name variation; Birjis Qadr was the minor son, not the leader executing operations.

12. **Which administrative change directly followed the suppression of the Revolt of 1857?**
   - A) The introduction of the Dual Government system
   - B) The end of the East India Company's rule and transfer of power to the British Crown
   - C) The creation of the post of Governor-General of India
   - D) The complete independence of princely states
   - **Answer: B**
   - Explanation: The Government of India Act 1858 abolished the East India Company and transferred the governance of India directly to the British Crown. Why others are wrong: The Dual Government was introduced by Robert Clive in 1765; the post of Governor-General was created by the Regulating Act of 1773; princely states remained under British paramountcy rather than gaining full independence.

13. **What was the title given to the Governor-General of India after the power transfer to the British Crown in 1858?**
   - A) Viceroy
   - B) Secretary of State
   - C) High Commissioner
   - D) Governor-Provincial
   - **Answer: A**
   - Explanation: Under the Government of India Act 1858, the Governor-General was given the additional title of Viceroy, serving as the direct representative of the Crown. Why others are wrong: The Secretary of State was a British cabinet minister based in London; High Commissioner and Governor-Provincial were different administrative titles used in other contexts.

14. **Mangal Pandey, who fired the first shot of defiance against British officers, belonged to which infantry regiment?**
   - A) 19th Native Infantry
   - B) 34th Native Infantry
   - C) 3rd Bengal Light Cavalry
   - D) 7th Awadh Regiment
   - **Answer: B**
   - Explanation: Mangal Pandey was a sepoy in the 34th Bengal Native Infantry stationed at Barrackpore. Why others are wrong: The 19th Native Infantry at Berhampur refused the cartridges earlier but didn't resort to violence first; the 3rd Cavalry mutinied at Meerut; the 7th Awadh rose later.

15. **Which influential religious leader, known as the 'Lighthouse of Rebellion', successfully led forces in the Faizabad region?**
   - A) Shah Mal
   - B) Maulvi Ahmadullah Shah
   - C) Liaquat Ali
   - D) Khan Bahadur Khan
   - **Answer: B**
   - Explanation: Maulvi Ahmadullah Shah of Faizabad was an outstanding leader of the revolt who mobilized forces and engaged British troops in heavy combat. Why others are wrong: Shah Mal led peasants in Baghpat; Liaquat Ali led at Allahabad; Khan Bahadur Khan led at Bareilly.

16. **Following the failure of the revolt, what was the fate of the Mughal Emperor Bahadur Shah Zafar?**
   - A) He was publicly executed in Delhi
   - B) He was exiled to Rangoon (Burma)
   - C) He was allowed to stay in the Red Fort as a pensioner
   - D) He escaped to Nepal
   - **Answer: B**
   - Explanation: Bahadur Shah Zafar was tried by the British, stripped of his titles, and exiled to Rangoon, where he passed away in 1862. Why others are wrong: He was not executed, though his sons were; he was not allowed to remain in Delhi; Nana Sahib and Begum Hazrat Mahal escaped to Nepal, not Zafar.

17. **How did the composition of the British Indian Army change in terms of recruitment after the 1857 revolt?**
   - A) Increased recruitment from Bihar and Awadh
   - B) Increased recruitment from Punjab, Nepal, and the North-West Frontier (Martial Races)
   - C) Complete exclusion of Indian soldiers from the infantry
   - D) Recruitment solely from the Madras and Bombay presidencies
   - **Answer: B**
   - Explanation: The Peel Commission recommended shifting recruitment away from the rebellious high-caste regions of Awadh and Bihar toward regions that helped suppress the revolt, branding them as 'martial races'. Why others are wrong: Recruitment from Bihar and Awadh was severely reduced; Indians were still needed in the infantry due to sheer size requirements; Madras and Bombay remained but Punjab and Gurkhas became dominant.

18. **What assurance was given to the Indian Princely States by Queen Victoria's Proclamation of 1858?**
   - A) They would be systematically annexed into British India
   - B) The Doctrine of Lapse was abandoned, and their territorial integrity would be respected
   - C) They would receive complete military autonomy
   - D) They would no longer pay taxes to the Crown
   - **Answer: B**
   - Explanation: Queen Victoria's Proclamation sought to appease the native rulers by promising that the British would respect their rights and refrain from further territorial annexation or applying the Doctrine of Lapse. Why others are wrong: Annexation policy was abandoned; military autonomy was not granted; they still had to acknowledge British paramountcy and financial obligations.

19. **Which social reform introduced by the British generated fear among traditional classes that their religion was under threat?**
   - A) The Hindu Widows' Remarriage Act of 1856
   - B) The Regulating Act of 1773
   - C) The Vernacular Press Act
   - D) The Rowlatt Act
   - **Answer: A**
   - Explanation: The legalization of Hindu widow remarriage in 1856, alongside the abolition of Sati (1829), was seen by conservative sections of society as unwarranted interference in their religious and social traditions. Why others are wrong: The Regulating Act was political; the Vernacular Press Act (1878) and Rowlatt Act (1919) were post-1857 political legislations.

20. **The Lex Loci Act of 1850 caused alarm among orthodox Indians because it provided what right?**
   - A) Right of Christian converts to inherit ancestral property
   - B) Right of women to vote
   - C) Exemption of Brahmins from prison sentences
   - D) Free passage for pilgrims
   - **Answer: A**
   - Explanation: The Lex Loci Act enabled a person who converted to Christianity to inherit their ancestral property, which traditional elements viewed as an open incentive for conversion. Why others are wrong: Women's voting rights, Brahmin exemptions, and pilgrim free passages were not part of this property-focused law.

21. **Who was the British Prime Minister during the outbreak of the Revolt of 1857?**
   - A) Benjamin Disraeli
   - B) Lord Palmerston
   - C) William Gladstone
   - D) Robert Peel
   - **Answer: B**
   - Explanation: Henry John Temple, 3rd Viscount Palmerston (Lord Palmerston), was the Prime Minister of the United Kingdom when the mutiny broke out. Why others are wrong: Disraeli and Gladstone served as PMs later in the Victorian era; Robert Peel was PM in the 1840s.

22. **Which external global conflict immediately preceding 1857 shattered the myth of British military invincibility in the minds of Indian sepoys?**
   - A) The Crimean War
   - B) The Napoleonic Wars
   - C) The First World War
   - D) The Opium Wars
   - **Answer: A**
   - Explanation: Severe British losses in the Crimean War (1853â€“1856) and the First Afghan War showed Indian soldiers that the British army could be beaten. Why others are wrong: The Napoleonic Wars ended much earlier in 1815; the First World War happened in the 20th century; the Opium Wars resulted in easy British victories over China.

23. **Who among the following leaders of the revolt escaped into the jungles of Nepal and was never captured?**
   - A) Tantia Tope
   - B) Nana Sahib
   - C) Kunwar Singh
   - D) Bakht Khan
   - **Answer: B**
   - Explanation: Nana Sahib, the adopted son of Peshwa Baji Rao II, refused to surrender and escaped into the foothill jungles of Nepal after the fall of Kanpur. Why others are wrong: Tantia Tope was betrayed and hanged; Kunwar Singh died of battlefield injuries; Bakht Khan died in action.

24. **What was the main reason for the failure of the Revolt of 1857 despite its widespread nature?**
   - A) Complete lack of modern weapons among the sepoys
   - B) Lack of unified leadership, common purpose, and participation from all regions of India
   - C) Instantaneous suppression by the Royal Air Force
   - D) Immediate intervention by French forces assisting the British
   - **Answer: B**
   - Explanation: The revolt was localized, failing to encompass Southern and Western India, and lacked a single central leader or forward-looking political vision, with different groups fighting for individual grievances. Why others are wrong: Sepoys used captured modern British weaponry; aircraft did not exist; French forces did not participate in suppressing the internal Indian mutiny.

25. **Which modern Indian historian famously categorized the 1857 uprising as the "First War of Indian Independence"?**
   - A) R.C. Majumdar
   - B) Vinayak Damodar Savarkar
   - C) S.N. Sen
   - D) Bipan Chandra
   - **Answer: B**
   - Explanation: V.D. Savarkar published 'The Indian War of Independence of 1857' in 1909, framing the mutiny as a unified nationalist struggle against foreign rule. Why others are wrong: R.C. Majumdar famously argued it was neither first, nor national, nor a war of independence; S.N. Sen wrote the official history 'Eighteen Fifty-Seven'; Bipan Chandra was a later Marxist/Nationalist historian.

26. **The city of Allahabad was led during the 1857 revolt by which local figure?**
   - A) Khan Bahadur
   - B) Maulvi Liaqat Ali
   - C) Azimullah
   - D) Shah Mal
   - **Answer: B**
   - Explanation: Maulvi Liaqat Ali was a school teacher who took over the administration of Allahabad during the uprising and turned it into a rebel stronghold. Why others are wrong: Khan Bahadur led at Bareilly; Azimullah was a diplomat for Nana Sahib; Shah Mal operated in the Meerut/Baghpat rural belt.

27. **Which major social class in India actively remained loyal to the British or aloof from the rebellion?**
   - A) The peasantry
   - B) The modern educated middle class
   - C) The high-caste sepoys
   - D) The dispossessed artisans
   - **Answer: B**
   - Explanation: The newly emerged modern educated Indian intelligentsia viewed the rebellion as backward-looking and believed British rule would modernize India. Why others are wrong: The peasantry, sepoys, and artisans formed the core fighting and supporting blocks of the rebellion due to economic ruin.

28. **What happened to the structure of the Board of Control and Court of Directors of the East India Company in 1858?**
   - A) Their powers were doubled
   - B) They were abolished and replaced by the Council of the Secretary of State
   - C) They were merged into the Indian National Congress
   - D) They were moved to Calcutta
   - **Answer: B**
   - Explanation: The dual system of control via the Board of Control and Court of Directors was scrapped, replaced by the Secretary of State for India assisted by a 15-member Council. Why others are wrong: Their powers were ended, not doubled; the Congress was formed much later in 1885; the institutions were liquidated entirely.

29. **Sir Hugh Rose described which rebel figure as "the bravest and best military leader of the rebels"?**
   - A) Nana Sahib
   - B) Tantia Tope
   - C) Rani Lakshmibai
   - D) Bahadur Shah Zafar
   - **Answer: C**
   - Explanation: Sir Hugh Rose, the British commander who fought against Rani Lakshmibai during the siege of Jhansi and Gwalior, paid this tribute to her bravery in his field dispatches. Why others are wrong: He did not use these specific words for Nana Sahib, Tantia Tope, or the elderly emperor Zafar.

30. **Which document officially declared the end of East India Company rule and announced the new policies of the British Crown toward India on November 1, 1858?**
   - A) The Regulating Act
   - B) Queen Victoria's Proclamation
   - C) The Ilbert Bill
   - D) The Pitts India Act
   - **Answer: B**
   - Explanation: Read by Lord Canning at a grand Durbar in Allahabad, Queen Victoria's Proclamation formalized the direct assumption of the Indian administration by the British monarch. Why others are wrong: The Regulating Act and Pitt's India Act were older laws managing the Company; the Ilbert Bill was an 1883 judicial controversy.

## Modern - Freedom Movement (INC Sessions, Moderates, Extremists, Revolutionary Movements) (Modern)

1.  Topic Introduction
    The provided MCQs center on the Modern Indian History segment of the General Awareness syllabus for the SSC CGL exam, specifically focusing on the Indian Freedom Movement. This covers the foundation and evolution of the Indian National Congress (INC), the ideological and strategic rift between the Moderates and Extremists, and the emergence of radical Revolutionary Movements both within India and abroad. Understanding these distinct phases and their respective leaders is crucial for scoring well in the exam.

2.  Core Concepts Explained Simply
    
      - Indian National Congress (INC) Foundations: Established in 1885 as a common political platform for educated Indians to voice grievances to the British administration through constitutional means.
      - Moderates vs. Extremists: Moderates (1885â€“1905) believed in British justice and constitutional agitation, relying on peaceful methods. Extremists (1905â€“1917) lost faith in British goodwill and advocated for Swaraj (self-rule) via passive resistance, Swadeshi (indigenous goods), and mass boycotts.
      - Revolutionary Movements: Militant nationalist groups who believed that colonial rule could only be overthrown by armed rebellion, secret societies, and targeted assassinations of oppressive British officials.

3.  Important Facts and Dates
    
      - 1885: First INC Session in Bombay, presided over by W. C. Bonnerjee and initiated by retired civil servant A. O. Hume.
      - 1904: Foundation of the Abhinav Bharat Society by V. D. Savarkar.
      - 1907: The Surat Split where the INC divided into Moderates and Extremists over leadership choices and expanding the Swadeshi movement.
      - 1911: Annulment of the Partition of Bengal announced at the Delhi Durbar by King George V.
      - 1913: Formation of the Ghadar Party in San Francisco by Lala Har Dayal and Sohan Singh Bhakna.
      - 1916: The Lucknow Pact which reunited the INC factions and formed an alliance with the Muslim League.
      - 1925: The Kakori Train Robbery executed by the Hindustan Republican Association (HRA).
      - 1929: The Lahore Session, presided over by Jawaharlal Nehru, where the resolution for 'Purna Swaraj' (Complete Independence) was adopted.

4.  Related Concepts Frequently Asked in SSC Exams
    
      - Key INC Presidents: Annie Besant was the first woman president (1917, Calcutta); Sarojini Naidu was the first Indian woman president (1925, Kanpur); Mahatma Gandhi presided only once (1924, Belgaum).
      - Revolutionary Phase II: The revival of revolutionary activities in the 1920s saw the transformation of HRA into the Hindustan Socialist Republican Association (HSRA) in 1928 under Bhagat Singh and Chandrashekhar Azad, shifting towards socialist ideologies.
      - Economic Critique: Early nationalists like Dadabhai Naoroji propounded the 'Drain of Wealth' theory in his book 'Poverty and Un-British Rule in India', exposing how colonial policies economically drained the country.

5.  Differences Between Similar Terms
    
      - Moderates Strategy vs. Extremists Strategy: Moderates utilized 'Prayer, Petition, and Protest' confined strictly to legal boundaries, while Extremists actively promoted 'Boycott, Swadeshi, and National Education' to turn it into an all-India mass struggle.
      - HRA vs. HSRA: The Hindustan Republican Association (HRA) was formed in 1924 focused primarily on republican ideals, whereas the Hindustan Socialist Republican Association (HSRA) was its successor formed in 1928, explicitly incorporating 'Socialist' ideology into its agenda.
      - First Woman President vs. First Indian Woman President: Annie Besant (British) holds the title of the first female president of the INC, whereas Sarojini Naidu holds the title of the first native Indian female president.

6.  Examples
    
      - Ideological Leaders: The Extremist faction was famously spearheaded by the 'Lal-Bal-Pal' trioâ€”Lala Lajpat Rai, Bal Gangadhar Tilak, and Bipin Chandra Pal.
      - Mass Mobilization: Bal Gangadhar Tilak utilized traditional cultural celebrations like the Shivaji Utsav and Ganesha Festival to bypass British bans on political gatherings and foster national unity.
      - Nationalist Press: Surendranath Banerjee edited 'The Bengali' (Moderate perspective), while Tilak edited 'Kesari' in Marathi and 'Mahratta' in English (Extremist perspective) to disseminate anti-colonial thoughts.

7.  Previous-Year Exam Perspective
    
      - SSC CGL exams regularly target factual matrixes concerning historical sessions (e.g., associating Lahore 1929 with Purna Swaraj or Karachi 1931 with Fundamental Rights). There is also a strong emphasis on revolutionary history, frequently testing the founders of secret societies (like Anushilan Samiti in Bengal), major conspiracy cases (Alipore or Delhi cases), and operational locations of overseas organizations like the Ghadar Party or India House.

8.  Quick Revision Points and Mnemonics
    
      - Surat Split: 1907 (Split sounds like Seven/07â€”the year they split).
      - Lucknow Reunion: 1916 (Lucknow brought 'Luck' back to unity).
      - Lal-Bal-Pal: Lajpat Rai (Punjab), Tilak (Maharashtra), Pal (Bengal).
      - First INC Session: 1885, Bombay, W. C. Bonnerjee (WC = Welcome Congress).
      - Purna Swaraj: 1929 Lahore Session, Jawaharlal Nehru.
      - Ghadar Party: San Francisco, USA, 1913, Lala Har Dayal.

### Quick Practice (30 MCQs)

1. **Who presided over the first session of the Indian National Congress held in Bombay in 1885?**
   - A) Dinshaw Wacha
   - B) Womesh Chandra Bonnerjee
   - C) Dadabhai Naoroji
   - D) Surendranath Banerjee
   - **Answer: B**
   - Explanation: The first session of the Indian National Congress in December 1885 was presided over by Womesh Chandra Bonnerjee and attended by 72 delegates. Why others are wrong: Dinshaw Wacha was a key founder but did not preside over the first session; Dadabhai Naoroji presided over the second session in 1886; Surendranath Banerjee did not attend the first session due to a concurrent meeting of the Indian National Conference.

2. **In which historic session did the Indian National Congress split into Moderates and Extremists?**
   - A) 1905 Benaras Session
   - B) 1906 Calcutta Session
   - C) 1907 Surat Session
   - D) 1916 Lucknow Session
   - **Answer: C**
   - Explanation: The 1907 Surat session, presided over by Rash Behari Ghosh, saw a severe rift over leadership choices and the extension of the Boycott movement, resulting in the Surat Split. Why others are wrong: The 1905 Benaras session expressed resentment against the partition of Bengal under G K Gokhale; the 1906 Calcutta session adopted Swaraj as the goal under Dadabhai Naoroji; the 1916 Lucknow session reunited both factions.

3. **Who among the following was considered the main political guru of Mahatma Gandhi and a prominent Moderate leader?**
   - A) Bal Gangadhar Tilak
   - B) Gopal Krishna Gokhale
   - C) Lala Lajpat Rai
   - D) Bipin Chandra Pal
   - **Answer: B**
   - Explanation: Gopal Krishna Gokhale was a leading Moderate who founded the Servants of India Society in 1905 and advocated for constitutional methods, serving as Gandhi's political mentor. Why others are wrong: Bal Gangadhar Tilak, Lala Lajpat Rai, and Bipin Chandra Pal formed the famous 'Lal-Bal-Pal' trio, who were leading pillars of the Extremist faction favoring passive resistance.

4. **The resolution on 'Purna Swaraj' or Complete Independence was adopted in which session of the Indian National Congress?**
   - A) 1920 Nagpur Session
   - B) 1929 Lahore Session
   - C) 1931 Karachi Session
   - D) 1938 Haripura Session
   - **Answer: B**
   - Explanation: The 1929 Lahore session, presided over by Jawaharlal Nehru, passed the historic resolution declaring Purna Swaraj as the ultimate goal of the Congress. Why others are wrong: The 1920 Nagpur session ratified the Non-Cooperation movement; the 1931 Karachi session adopted resolutions on Fundamental Rights and Economic Policy; the 1938 Haripura session was presided over by Subhas Chandra Bose.

5. **Who founded the revolutionary organization 'Abhinav Bharat Society' in 1904?**
   - A) Vinayak Damodar Savarkar
   - B) Barindra Kumar Ghosh
   - C) Khudiram Bose
   - D) Bhagat Singh
   - **Answer: A**
   - Explanation: V D Savarkar along with his brother Ganesh Savarkar founded the Abhinav Bharat Society as a secret revolutionary society initially started as Mitra Mela. Why others are wrong: Barindra Kumar Ghosh was associated with Anushilan Samiti; Khudiram Bose was an independent young revolutionary executed for the Muzaffarpur bombing; Bhagat Singh founded the Naujawan Bharat Sabha.

6. **Which of the following methods best describes the strategy used by the Moderate leaders of the INC?**
   - A) Boycott of foreign goods and institutions
   - B) Constitutional agitation and the method of 'Prayer, Petition, and Protest'
   - C) Armed insurrections against colonial offices
   - D) Organizing mass general strikes
   - **Answer: B**
   - Explanation: Moderates believed in constitutionalism and British justice, relying heavily on sending petitions, holding speeches, and passing peaceful resolutions. Why others are wrong: Boycott was an Extremist methodology; armed insurrections were characteristic of Revolutionary Movements; mass general strikes were popularized much later during Gandhian mass movements.

7. **The Anushilan Samiti, a prominent secret revolutionary society of the early 20th century, was founded in which province?**
   - A) Bombay
   - B) Punjab
   - C) Bengal
   - D) Madras
   - **Answer: C**
   - Explanation: The Anushilan Samiti was founded in Bengal by Pramathanath Mitra, Barindra Kumar Ghosh, and Jatindranath Banerjee to promote revolutionary activities. Why others are wrong: Bombay was a stronghold of Tilak's activities; Punjab saw revolutionary mobilization through the Ghadar party and Naujawan Bharat Sabha; Madras had the Bharatha Matha Society.

8. **Who was the first woman president of the Indian National Congress?**
   - A) Sarojini Naidu
   - B) Annie Besant
   - C) Nellie Sengupta
   - D) Kadambini Ganguly
   - **Answer: B**
   - Explanation: Annie Besant, the British socialist and Theosophist who started the Home Rule League, presided over the 1917 Calcutta session of the INC. Why others are wrong: Sarojini Naidu was the first Indian woman president (1925 Kanpur); Nellie Sengupta presided in 1933; Kadambini Ganguly was the first woman graduate to address the INC session.

9. **The Kakori Train Robbery of 1925 was organized by which revolutionary organization?**
   - A) Hindustan Republican Association (HRA)
   - B) Ghadar Party
   - C) Jugantar
   - D) Hindustan Socialist Republican Association (HSRA)
   - **Answer: A**
   - Explanation: The HRA, led by revolutionaries like Ram Prasad Bismil, Ashfaqullah Khan, and Chandrashekhar Azad, executed the train robbery at Kakori to fund their activities. Why others are wrong: The Ghadar Party operated overseas from San Francisco; Jugantar was an older Bengal revolutionary group; HSRA was the reformed version of HRA created later in 1928.

10. **Who famously proclaimed, 'Swaraj is my birthright and I shall have it'?**
   - A) Lala Lajpat Rai
   - B) Aurobindo Ghosh
   - C) Bal Gangadhar Tilak
   - D) Subhas Chandra Bose
   - **Answer: C**
   - Explanation: Lokmanya Bal Gangadhar Tilak, a towering leader of the Extremists, gave this slogan to ignite the spirit of self-rule during the anti-partition agitation. Why others are wrong: Lala Lajpat Rai was known as Punjab Kesari; Aurobindo Ghosh gave spiritual definitions to nationalism before retiring to Puducherry; Subhas Chandra Bose famously gave the slogan 'Jai Hind'.

11. **In which year did the historic Lucknow Pact take place, leading to the reunion of the Moderates and Extremists?**
   - A) 1907
   - B) 1911
   - C) 1916
   - D) 1920
   - **Answer: C**
   - Explanation: The 1916 Lucknow session, presided over by Ambica Charan Mazumdar, successfully reunited the Moderates and Extremists and forged an alliance with the Muslim League. Why others are wrong: 1907 was the year of the Surat split; 1911 marked the annulment of the partition of Bengal; 1920 was the launch year of the Non-Cooperation Movement.

12. **Who was the author of the famous book 'Poverty and Un-British Rule in India', which propounded the 'Drain Theory'?**
   - A) Romesh Chunder Dutt
   - B) Dadabhai Naoroji
   - C) M G Ranade
   - D) Gopal Krishna Gokhale
   - **Answer: B**
   - Explanation: Dadabhai Naoroji, known as the Grand Old Man of India, exposed the economic exploitation of India by the British through his Drain Theory in this book. Why others are wrong: R C Dutt wrote Economic History of India; M G Ranade was an economic analyst and judge; G K Gokhale was a Moderate leader who criticized imperial budgets but did not write this specific book.

13. **Which revolutionary was executed by the British authorities for assassinating British officer Curzon Wyllie in London in 1909?**
   - A) Madan Lal Dhingra
   - B) Udham Singh
   - C) Bhagat Singh
   - D) Surya Sen
   - **Answer: A**
   - Explanation: Madan Lal Dhingra, an associate of India House in London, assassinated Curzon Wyllie as a protest against the political execution of Indian nationalists. Why others are wrong: Udham Singh assassinated Michael O'Dwyer in 1940; Bhagat Singh was executed for the Lahore Conspiracy case; Surya Sen led the Chittagong Armoury Raid.

14. **Who was the president of the historic 1931 Karachi Session of the INC, which adopted the resolution on Fundamental Rights?**
   - A) Jawaharlal Nehru
   - B) Mahatma Gandhi
   - C) Vallabhbhai Patel
   - D) Subhas Chandra Bose
   - **Answer: C**
   - Explanation: Sardar Vallabhbhai Patel presided over the Karachi session in 1931, which approved the Gandhi-Irwin Pact and passed resolutions on economic policies. Why others are wrong: Jawaharlal Nehru presided over the 1929 Lahore and 1936 Lucknow sessions; Mahatma Gandhi presided only once, at the 1924 Belgaum session; Subhas Chandra Bose presided in 1938.

15. **The Alipore Bomb Conspiracy Case (1908) was launched against which prominent leaders for revolutionary activities in Bengal?**
   - A) Aurobindo Ghosh and Barindra Kumar Ghosh
   - B) Bhagat Singh and Sukhdev
   - C) Rash Behari Bose and Sachin Sanyal
   - D) Lala Har Dayal and Sohan Singh Bhakna
   - **Answer: A**
   - Explanation: The Alipore case arose following the attempt on Magistrate Kingsford's life, leading to the arrest of the Ghosh brothers and secret raid on the Muraripukur garden house. Why others are wrong: Bhagat Singh and Sukhdev were tried in the Lahore Conspiracy Case; Rash Behari Bose and Sachin Sanyal were involved in the Delhi-Lahore Conspiracy Case; Har Dayal and Bhakna founded the Ghadar Movement.

16. **What was the main reason behind the Extremists' objection to the Moderate leadership during the 1905-1907 period?**
   - A) Moderates wanted immediate armed rebellion
   - B) Moderates limited the Swadeshi and Boycott movements only to Bengal
   - C) Moderates supported the partition of Bengal
   - D) Moderates refused to participate in the legislative councils
   - **Answer: B**
   - Explanation: Extremists wanted to take the anti-partition Swadeshi movement outside Bengal to make it an all-India mass struggle, while Moderates wanted to confine it to Bengal and keep it strictly constitutional. Why others are wrong: Moderates never wanted armed rebellion; they strongly opposed the partition of Bengal; they actively favored participation in councils to reform them.

17. **The Ghadar Party, formed to overthrow British rule in India via an armed revolt, was established in which international city?**
   - A) London
   - B) Berlin
   - C) San Francisco
   - D) Tokyo
   - **Answer: C**
   - Explanation: The Ghadar Party was founded in 1913 by Lala Har Dayal, Sohan Singh Bhakna, and others in San Francisco, USA, operating out of the Yugantar Ashram. Why others are wrong: London hosted Shyamji Krishna Varma's India House; Berlin was home to the Indian Independence Committee during WWI; Tokyo hosted Rash Behari Bose in his later years.

18. **Who among the following is known as the 'Mother of Indian Revolution' for unfurling the first version of the Indian nationalist flag abroad?**
   - A) Bhikaiji Cama
   - B) Aruna Asaf Ali
   - C) Sarojini Naidu
   - D) Annie Besant
   - **Answer: A**
   - Explanation: Madam Bhikaiji Cama unfurled the Indian National Flag at the International Socialist Conference in Stuttgart, Germany, in 1907. Why others are wrong: Aruna Asaf Ali is famous for hoisting the flag during the Quit India Movement in 1942; Sarojini Naidu and Annie Besant were major political leaders within the INC structure.

19. **The Chittagong Armoury Raid of 1930 was planned and led by which legendary revolutionary?**
   - A) Chandrashekhar Azad
   - B) Surya Sen
   - C) Jatin Das
   - D) Bhagat Singh
   - **Answer: B**
   - Explanation: Surya Sen, popularly known as 'Masterda', led a group of revolutionaries to raid the police and auxiliary forces armouries in Chittagong to declare a provisional revolutionary government. Why others are wrong: Chandrashekhar Azad led the HSRA in Northern India; Jatin Das died during a historic 63-day hunger strike in prison; Bhagat Singh operated in Punjab and Delhi.

20. **Who was the Viceroy of India when the Indian National Congress was founded in 1885?**
   - A) Lord Curzon
   - B) Lord Dufferin
   - C) Lord Ripon
   - D) Lord Minto
   - **Answer: B**
   - Explanation: Lord Dufferin was the Viceroy of India from 1884 to 1888, during whose tenure the Congress held its inaugural session. Why others are wrong: Lord Curzon was responsible for the 1905 Partition of Bengal; Lord Ripon was the liberal Viceroy known for the local self-government resolution; Lord Minto was associated with the 1909 Morley-Minto reforms.

21. **Which INC session was the only one ever presided over by Mahatma Gandhi?**
   - A) 1920 Calcutta Session
   - B) 1924 Belgaum Session
   - C) 1925 Kanpur Session
   - D) 1930 Lahore Session
   - **Answer: B**
   - Explanation: Mahatma Gandhi presided over the 39th session of the Indian National Congress held at Belgaum, Karnataka, in December 1924. Why others are wrong: The 1920 Calcutta special session was presided by Lala Lajpat Rai; 1925 Kanpur was by Sarojini Naidu; there was no regular session in 1930 due to Civil Disobedience.

22. **The Hindustan Socialist Republican Association (HSRA) added the word 'Socialist' to its name in 1928 under the influence of which leader?**
   - A) Ram Prasad Bismil
   - B) Bhagat Singh
   - C) Sachin Sanyal
   - D) Jogesh Chandra Chatterji
   - **Answer: B**
   - Explanation: Bhagat Singh and Chandrashekhar Azad reorganised the HRA into the HSRA at Feroz Shah Kotla in Delhi, adopting socialism as their explicit ideological framework. Why others are wrong: Ram Prasad Bismil, Sachin Sanyal, and Jogesh Chandra Chatterji were founders of the original HRA, which focused primarily on republicanism before the socialist shift.

23. **Which Extremist leader edited the influential Marathi newspaper 'Kesari' to spread nationalist consciousness?**
   - A) Bipin Chandra Pal
   - B) Lala Lajpat Rai
   - C) Bal Gangadhar Tilak
   - D) Vishnu Shastri Chiplunkar
   - **Answer: C**
   - Explanation: Bal Gangadhar Tilak used his journals 'Kesari' (in Marathi) and 'Mahratta' (in English) to criticize colonial policies and mobilize the public. Why others are wrong: Bipin Chandra Pal edited 'New India'; Lala Lajpat Rai published 'The Punjabi'; Chiplunkar was a writer who co-founded the New English School but did not edit Kesari.

24. **Who among the following threw a bomb in the Central Legislative Assembly in New Delhi in 1908 along with Prafulla Chaki?**
   - A) Khudiram Bose
   - B) Bhagat Singh
   - C) Batukeshwar Dutt
   - D) Rajguru
   - **Answer: A**
   - Explanation: Khudiram Bose and Prafulla Chaki threw a bomb at a carriage in Muzaffarpur in 1908 intending to kill the unpopular judge Kingsford, not in the Central Assembly.
