Why others are wrong: Bhagat Singh and Batukeshwar Dutt threw bombs in the Central Legislative Assembly in 1929; Rajguru was involved in the assassination of Saunders.

25. **Who was the British retired civil servant who played an instrumental role in organizing the initial meetings of the Indian National Congress?**
   - A) Allan Octavian Hume
   - B) William Wedderburn
   - C) Henry Cotton
   - D) David Yule
   - **Answer: A**
   - Explanation: A. O. Hume is widely recognized as the founder of the Indian National Congress, acting as its General Secretary for the initial years to provide a platform for educated Indians. Why others are wrong: William Wedderburn and Henry Cotton were British sympathizers who presided over later Congress sessions; David Yule was the first European president of the INC (1888 Allahabad).

26. **The famous 'Delhi Conspiracy Case' of 1912 was an attempt on the life of which Viceroy of India?**
   - A) Lord Hardinge
   - B) Lord Chelmsford
   - C) Lord Irwin
   - D) Lord Linlithgow
   - **Answer: A**
   - Explanation: Revolutionary Rash Behari Bose along with Sachin Sanyal threw a bomb at Viceroy Lord Hardinge while he was making a state entry into Delhi. Why others are wrong: Lord Chelmsford was Viceroy during the Rowlatt Act; Lord Irwin was Viceroy during the Salt March; Lord Linlithgow served during World War II.

27. **Which publication was launched by the Moderate leader Surendranath Banerjee to advocate national integration?**
   - A) The Bengali
   - B) Voice of India
   - C) Young India
   - D) Harijan
   - **Answer: A**
   - Explanation: Surendranath Banerjee edited 'The Bengali', an influential newspaper that voiced the opinions of early liberal political thinkers in India. Why others are wrong: Voice of India was published by Dadabhai Naoroji; Young India and Harijan were journals edited by Mahatma Gandhi.

28. **What concrete step did the Extremists advocate during the Anti-Partition movement that Moderates hesitated to fully endorse?**
   - A) Writing letters to British MPs
   - B) Organizing mass boycotts of government schools, courts, and jobs
   - C) Sending representations to the Privy Council
   - D) Celebrating traditional festivals
   - **Answer: B**
   - Explanation: Extremists demanded a full political boycott of British administration, including educational institutions and courts, moving beyond the simple boycott of foreign clothes favored by Moderates. Why others are wrong: Writing letters and sending representations were Moderate tactics; both groups supported using traditional festivals like Shivaji Utsav, though Tilak popularized it.

29. **In which year was the partition of Bengal officially revoked, a major victory for both Moderates and Extremists?**
   - A) 1909
   - B) 1911
   - C) 1919
   - D) 1922
   - **Answer: B**
   - Explanation: King George V announced the annulment of the partition of Bengal at the Delhi Durbar held in December 1911. Why others are wrong: 1909 saw the introduction of the Morley-Minto reforms; 1919 was the year of the Jallianwala Bagh massacre; 1922 was the suspension of the Non-Cooperation Movement.

30. **The 'Indian Home Rule Society' and 'India House' in London were established by which Indian revolutionary nationalist?**
   - A) Shyamji Krishna Varma
   - B) Lala Har Dayal
   - C) V. D. Savarkar
   - D) Rash Behari Bose
   - **Answer: A**
   - Explanation: Shyamji Krishna Varma founded India House and the Indian Home Rule Society in London in 1905 to serve as a base for radical nationalist students studying in Britain. Why others are wrong: Lala Har Dayal moved to the US to join Ghadar; Savarkar was a resident of India House but not its original founder; Rash Behari Bose operated primarily within India and later Japan.

## Modern - Freedom Movement (Gandhian Era, Major Movements, Round Table Conferences) (Modern)

1.  Topic Introduction
    The provided questions center around Modern Indian History, specifically focusing on the Freedom Movement, the Gandhian Era (1915â€“1947), major mass movements, and the Round Table Conferences. This crucial historical period details India's organized transition from moderate constitutional agitation to radical revolutionary activities and massive non-violent popular resistance led by Mahatma Gandhi. It represents one of the highest-weightage topics within the General Awareness section of the SSC CGL exam.

2.  Core Concepts Explained Simply  - Gandhian Satyagraha: A philosophy of peaceful resistance rooted strictly in Satya (truth) and Ahimsa (non-violence). It targeted the conscience of the oppressor rather than inflicting physical injury.
  - Mass Movements: Nationwide agitations organized to mobilize all sections of Indian society against colonial rule. The three primary milestones were the Non-Cooperation Movement (1920), Civil Disobedience Movement (1930), and Quit India Movement (1942).
  - Round Table Conferences: Three formal constitutional dialogue sessions held in London between British politicians and various Indian political leaders to deliberate on future governance and administrative reforms in India.
  - Electorate Debates: Disagreements over electoral mechanisms, notably separate electorates (where only members of a specific community vote for candidates from their own community) versus joint electorates with reserved seats.3.  Important Facts and Dates  - 1915: Mahatma Gandhi returns to India from South Africa on January 9 (celebrated as Pravasi Bharatiya Divas).
  - 1917: Champaran Satyagraha (Gandhi's first active civil disobedience movement in India against the Tinkathia system).
  - 1918: Kheda Satyagraha (tax relief struggle) and Ahmedabad Mill Strike (first hunger strike).
  - 1920 (August): Launch of the Non-Cooperation Movement; suspended in February 1922 due to the Chauri Chaura incident.
  - 1924: Belgaum Sessionâ€”the only INC session presided over by Mahatma Gandhi.
  - 1929: Lahore Session of the INC, where the Purna Swaraj (Complete Independence) resolution was passed under Jawaharlal Nehru.
  - 1930 (March 12 â€“ April 6): Dandi March (Salt Satyagraha) consisting of Gandhi and 78 delegates, initiating the Civil Disobedience Movement.
  - 1930, 1931, 1932: First, Second, and Third Round Table Conferences. The INC only participated in the Second.
  - 1931 (March 5): Signing of the Gandhi-Irwin Pact.
  - 1932 (September): Signing of the Poona Pact between Dr. B.R. Ambedkar and Mahatma Gandhi's representatives.
  - 1940 (October): Launch of Individual Satyagraha; Acharya Vinoba Bhave was chosen as the first satyagrahi.
  - 1942 (August 8): Launch of the Quit India Movement with the mantra "Do or Die".4.  Related Concepts Frequently Asked in SSC Exams  - Revolutionary Nationalism: Paralleling the Gandhian movements, organizations like the Hindustan Socialist Republican Association (HSRA) led by Bhagat Singh, Chandrashekhar Azad, and Sukhdev conducted high-profile actions against colonial targets.
  - Pre-Gandhian Political Mentors: SSC regularly asks about foundational political networks. Gopal Krishna Gokhale acted as Gandhiâ€™s political mentor, while Dadabhai Naoroji served as a foundational guide to early nationalists.
  - British Missions & Proposals: Key constitutional milestones include the Simon Commission (1928), the August Offer (1940), the Cripps Mission (1942), the Wavell Plan (1945), and the Cabinet Mission Plan (1946).
  - Important Publications: Publications started by Gandhi include Indian Opinion (South Africa), Young India, Navajivan, and Harijan (1933).5.  Differences Between Similar Terms  - Non-Cooperation vs. Civil Disobedience: Non-Cooperation (1920) aimed at refusing to associate with the colonial government by boycotting titles, courts, and schools while remaining within the law. Civil Disobedience (1930) went a step further by actively breaking specific colonial laws, such as the salt manufacturing monopoly.
  - Separate Electorate vs. Reserved Seats: A separate electorate meant only voters of a specific community could vote for candidates of that community, structurally isolating groups. Reserved seats meant the seat was earmarked for a specific community, but all voters in that constituency cast their ballots together under a joint electorate.
  - Simon Commission vs. Cripps Mission: The Simon Commission (1928) was sent to review the working of the Government of India Act 1919 and recommend constitutional updates. The Cripps Mission (1942) came to negotiate immediate Indian cooperation during World War II in exchange for post-war dominion status.6.  Examples  - Application of Ahimsa: The abrupt withdrawal of the nationwide Non-Cooperation Movement in 1922 following the Chauri Chaura incident demonstrates Gandhi's absolute prioritization of non-violence over political momentum.
  - Underground Resistance: During the Quit India Movement, when all senior leaders were instantly jailed, activists like Usha Mehta ran the "Secret Congress Radio," while Aruna Asaf Ali hoisted the flag at Gowalia Tank Maidan to keep the public struggle active.
  - Impact of Literature: John Ruskin's book 'Unto This Last' deeply influenced Gandhi's economic principles regarding the dignity of labor and communal life, which he practiced at Phoenix Settlement and Tolstoy Farm in South Africa.7.  Previous-Year Exam Perspective
    SSC CGL exams frequently test exact historical chronologies, key slogans, and administrative titles. Questions commonly focus on matching specific movements to their triggering incidents (e.g., Jallianwala Bagh Massacre triggering Non-Cooperation, or the failure of the Cripps Mission triggering Quit India). Additionally, examiners often target specific statistics (such as the 78 delegates on the Dandi March), unique session presidencies (like Gandhi at Belgaum in 1924), and names of individuals who attended all three Round Table Conferences (Dr. B.R. Ambedkar and Tej Bahadur Sapru).

8.  Quick Revision Points and Mnemonics  - Early Struggles Chronology: CAK = Champaran (1917), Ahmedabad (1918), Kheda (1918).
  - Only Gandhi Presidency: Belgaum 1924 (Mnemonic: Gandhi rang the "Bell" at Belgaum once).
  - Three Conferences: Ambedkar attended 1, 2, and 3; Gandhi/INC attended ONLY 2.
  - Satyagrahis Order: 1st = Vinoba Bhave, 2nd = Jawaharlal Nehru, 3rd = Brahma Datt.
  - Slogans: Do or Die = Gandhi (1942); Jai Hind = Subhas Chandra Bose; Inquilab Zindabad = Bhagat Singh (popularized).

### Quick Practice (30 MCQs)

1. **Who among the following is widely regarded as the political guru of Mahatma Gandhi during the early phase of the Indian Freedom Movement?**
   - A) Bal Gangadhar Tilak
   - B) Gopal Krishna Gokhale
   - C) Dadabhai Naoroji
   - D) Chittaranjan Das
   - **Answer: B**
   - Explanation: Gopal Krishna Gokhale was a senior leader of the Indian National Congress and the founder of the Servants of India Society. Upon Gandhi's return from South Africa, Gokhale advised him to travel across India to understand the socio-political realities before actively joining the movement, serving as his political mentor.
Why others are wrong: Bal Gangadhar Tilak was a prominent extremist leader whose radical political approach differed significantly from Gandhi's; Dadabhai Naoroji was known as the 'Grand Old Man of India' and a mentor to many, but not specifically Gandhi's political guru; Chittaranjan Das was a leader of the Swaraj Party and mentored Subhas Chandra Bose.

2. **In which year did Mahatma Gandhi launch the Champaran Satyagraha, marking his first major active movement in India?**
   - A) 1915
   - B) 1916
   - C) 1917
   - D) 1918
   - **Answer: C**
   - Explanation: The Champaran Satyagraha of 1917 was the first Satyagraha movement led by Mahatma Gandhi in India. It was launched in the Champaran district of Bihar to protest against the oppressive Tinkathia system, under which tenant farmers were forced to cultivate indigo.
Why others are wrong: In 1915, Gandhi returned to India from South Africa; in 1916, he attended the Lucknow Session of the Congress and travelled to study the country; in 1918, he led the Kheda Satyagraha and the Ahmedabad Mill Strike.

3. **Which specific grievance led to Mahatma Gandhi organizing the Kheda Satyagraha in 1918?**
   - A) Excessive tax assessment during a severe famine
   - B) Low wages of mill workers during a plague epidemic
   - C) Forced cultivation of indigo on agricultural lands
   - D) Strict restrictions on salt manufacturing by locals
   - **Answer: A**
   - Explanation: The Kheda Satyagraha of 1918 was organized by Gandhi in support of the peasants of Kheda district in Gujarat, who were unable to pay the high land revenue demanded by the British authorities due to a severe crop failure and famine.
Why others are wrong: Low wages during a plague epidemic led to the Ahmedabad Mill Strike; forced indigo cultivation was the cause of the Champaran Satyagraha; salt manufacturing restrictions triggered the Civil Disobedience Movement much later in 1930.

4. **What major event prompted Mahatma Gandhi to launch the nationwide Non-Cooperation Movement in 1920?**
   - A) The passing of the Government of India Act 1919
   - B) The Jallianwala Bagh Massacre and the Khilafat wrong
   - C) The execution of Bhagat Singh, Sukhdev, and Rajguru
   - D) The arrival of the Simon Commission in India
   - **Answer: B**
   - Explanation: The Non-Cooperation Movement was launched by Mahatma Gandhi in August 1920 in response to the Jallianwala Bagh Massacre, the harsh terms imposed on Turkey after WWI (the Khilafat issue), and the repressive Rowlatt Act, aiming for Swaraj.
Why others are wrong: The Government of India Act 1919 led to internal constitutional debates but not immediate total non-cooperation; Bhagat Singh and his comrades were executed later in 1931; the Simon Commission arrived in India in 1928, sparking widespread protests but after the Non-Cooperation era.

5. **Why did Mahatma Gandhi abruptly suspend the Non-Cooperation Movement in February 1922?**
   - A) The British government agreed to grant complete independence
   - B) The movement failed to garner public support across rural areas
   - C) The violent incident at Chauri Chaura where a police station was burned
   - D) The arrest of all prominent Congress leaders by the authorities
   - **Answer: C**
   - Explanation: Gandhi, a strict follower of Ahimsa (non-violence), called off the Non-Cooperation Movement following the Chauri Chaura incident in Gorakhpur district, Uttar Pradesh, where an angry mob set fire to a police station, killing 22 policemen.
Why others are wrong: The British did not grant independence at this stage; the movement had massive public participation; Gandhi chose to suspend it voluntarily due to violence, rather than being forced by mass arrests.

6. **Who presided over the historic 1924 Belgaum Session of the Indian National Congress, which was unique in its leadership?**
   - A) Jawaharlal Nehru
   - B) Subhas Chandra Bose
   - C) Mahatma Gandhi
   - D) Vallabhbhai Patel
   - **Answer: C**
   - Explanation: The 1924 Belgaum Session was the only Indian National Congress session ever presided over by Mahatma Gandhi himself.
Why others are wrong: Jawaharlal Nehru presided over several crucial sessions like Lahore (1929); Subhas Chandra Bose presided over Haripura (1938) and Tripuri (1939); Vallabhbhai Patel presided over the Karachi Session (1931).

7. **The famous Dandi March, initiated by Mahatma Gandhi on March 12, 1930, marked the formal launch of which major national movement?**
   - A) Non-Cooperation Movement
   - B) Civil Disobedience Movement
   - C) Quit India Movement
   - D) Swadeshi Movement
   - **Answer: B**
   - Explanation: The Dandi March, also known as the Salt Satyagraha, was a direct action campaign of tax resistance and nonviolent protest against the British salt monopoly, which officially began the nationwide Civil Disobedience Movement.
Why others are wrong: The Non-Cooperation Movement occurred earlier (1920-1922); the Quit India Movement was launched later in 1942; the Swadeshi Movement took place much earlier in 1905 against the partition of Bengal.

8. **How many delegates accompanied Mahatma Gandhi on the initial journey from Sabarmati Ashram to Dandi during the Salt March?**
   - A) 50 delegates
   - B) 78 delegates
   - C) 100 delegates
   - D) 200 delegates
   - **Answer: B**
   - Explanation: Gandhi began the march from his Sabarmati Ashram with 78 chosen satyagrahis or delegates representing different regions and communities of India, marching 241 miles to the coastal village of Dandi.
Why others are wrong: 50, 100, and 200 are incorrect estimates; the historical records specify exactly 78 companions at the commencement of the march.

9. **Who among the following British Prime Ministers chaired and announced the First Round Table Conference in London?**
   - A) Winston Churchill
   - B) Ramsay MacDonald
   - C) Clement Attlee
   - D) Neville Chamberlain
   - **Answer: B**
   - Explanation: Ramsay MacDonald was the British Prime Minister who formally opened the First Round Table Conference in November 1930 to discuss constitutional reforms in India.
Why others are wrong: Winston Churchill was a fierce opponent of Indian independence during this era; Clement Attlee was the Prime Minister who finally oversaw independence in 1947; Neville Chamberlain was Prime Minister during the late 1930s leading up to WWII.

10. **Why did the Indian National Congress boycott the First Round Table Conference held in 1930?**
   - A) The British refused to invite any Indian representatives
   - B) Most prominent Congress leaders were imprisoned during the Civil Disobedience Movement
   - C) The Muslim League refused to attend if Congress participated
   - D) The venue was shifted from Delhi to London against Indian wishes
   - **Answer: B**
   - Explanation: The Congress boycotted the First Round Table Conference because its top leadership and thousands of volunteers were jailed as a result of the ongoing Civil Disobedience Movement launched earlier that year.
Why others are wrong: British authorities did invite Indians, and many princely states and minor parties attended; the Muslim League actually participated in the first conference; the location was always planned for London.

11. **The signing of the historic Gandhi-Irwin Pact in March 1931 led to which immediate political consequence?**
   - A) The absolute dissolution of the British Raj in India
   - B) The suspension of the Civil Disobedience Movement and Congress agreement to attend the Second Round Table Conference
   - C) The immediate declaration of Purna Swaraj (Complete Independence)
   - D) The release of all political prisoners including those convicted of violent acts
   - **Answer: B**
   - Explanation: The Gandhi-Irwin Pact was a political agreement signed by Mahatma Gandhi and the Viceroy, Lord Irwin. Under this pact, Gandhi agreed to discontinue the Civil Disobedience Movement and attend the Second Round Table Conference, while the British agreed to release non-violent political prisoners.
Why others are wrong: The pact did not end British rule or declare Purna Swaraj; prisoners convicted of violence (like Bhagat Singh) were explicitly excluded from the release terms.

12. **Which was the only Round Table Conference in which Mahatma Gandhi formally participated as the sole representative of the Indian National Congress?**
   - A) First Round Table Conference
   - B) Second Round Table Conference
   - C) Third Round Table Conference
   - D) Fourth Round Table Conference
   - **Answer: B**
   - Explanation: Following the Gandhi-Irwin Pact, Gandhi travelled to London to attend the Second Round Table Conference (Septemberâ€“December 1931) as the singular representative of the Congress.
Why others are wrong: Congress boycotted both the First (1930) and the Third (1932) Round Table Conferences; there was no Fourth Round Table Conference.

13. **Who among the following Indian leaders attended all three Round Table Conferences held in London?**
   - A) Mahatma Gandhi
   - B) Dr. B.R. Ambedkar
   - C) Jawaharlal Nehru
   - D) Sardar Vallabhbhai Patel
   - **Answer: B**
   - Explanation: Dr. B.R. Ambedkar, representing the Depressed Classes, along with Tej Bahadur Sapru, attended all three Round Table Conferences to advocate for political safeguards for their respective constituencies.
Why others are wrong: Mahatma Gandhi only attended the second conference; Jawaharlal Nehru and Sardar Patel did not attend any of the conferences in London due to organizational strategies and imprisonments.

14. **What controversial measure announced by Ramsay MacDonald in 1932 led to Mahatma Gandhi undertaking a fast-unto-death in Yerwada Jail?**
   - A) The Rowlatt Act extension
   - B) The Communal Award granting separate electorates for Depressed Classes
   - C) The execution orders for revolutionary nationalists
   - D) The complete ban on public manufacture of domestic salt
   - **Answer: B**
   - Explanation: The Communal Award of 1932 proposed separate electorates not only for Muslims, Sikhs, and Europeans but also for the Depressed Classes (Dalits). Gandhi opposed this as he believed it would permanently divide Hindu society, leading him to fast in protest.
Why others are wrong: The Rowlatt Act was passed in 1919; executions of major revolutionaries occurred in 1931; salt restrictions were a 1930 issue that had already been partially addressed in local concessions.

15. **The Poona Pact of September 1932, which resolved the crisis over separate electorates, was signed between Mahatma Gandhi (via representatives) and which leader?**
   - A) Muhammad Ali Jinnah
   - B) Dr. B.R. Ambedkar
   - C) Lord Willingdon
   - D) Subhas Chandra Bose
   - **Answer: B**
   - Explanation: The Poona Pact was an agreement between Dr. B.R. Ambedkar and Mahatma Gandhi (signed on his behalf by Madan Mohan Malaviya) which abandoned separate electorates for the depressed classes but significantly increased the number of reserved seats for them in provincial legislatures.
Why others are wrong: Muhammad Ali Jinnah was the leader of the Muslim League and not a party to this social pact; Lord Willingdon was the Viceroy but not a signatory; Subhas Chandra Bose was not involved in these specific negotiations.

16. **During which historic session did the Indian National Congress pass the famous 'Quit India' Resolution under Gandhi's guidance?**
   - A) Lahore Session 1929
   - B) Karachi Session 1931
   - C) Bombay Session 1942
   - D) Haripura Session 1938
   - **Answer: C**
   - Explanation: The Quit India Resolution was passed by the All India Congress Committee at the Bombay session on August 8, 1942, launching a mass protest demanding an orderly British withdrawal from India.
Why others are wrong: The Lahore Session passed the Purna Swaraj resolution; the Karachi Session endorsed the Gandhi-Irwin Pact and fundamental rights; the Haripura Session dealt with internal planning and elected Subhas Chandra Bose.

17. **What famous slogan did Mahatma Gandhi give to the nation on the eve of launching the Quit India Movement in 1942?**
   - A) Jai Hind
   - B) Inquilab Zindabad
   - C) Do or Die (Karo ya Maro)
   - D) Swaraj is my birthright
   - **Answer: C**
   - Explanation: In his speech at the Gowalia Tank Maidan in Bombay, Gandhi delivered the mantra "Do or Die," calling on Indians to either free India or die in the attempt, showcasing a shift to a more resolute stance.
Why others are wrong: "Jai Hind" was popularized by Subhas Chandra Bose; "Inquilab Zindabad" was coined by Maulana Hasrat Mohani and popularized by Bhagat Singh; "Swaraj is my birthright" was the iconic declaration of Bal Gangadhar Tilak.

18. **Where was Mahatma Gandhi imprisoned by the British authorities immediately following the launch of the Quit India Movement in 1942?**
   - A) Cellular Jail, Andaman
   - B) Yerwada Central Jail, Pune
   - C) Aga Khan Palace, Pune
   - D) Naini Central Prison, Prayagraj
   - **Answer: C**
   - Explanation: Following the launch of the Quit India Movement, the British government immediately arrested all top Congress leaders under Operation Gulmarg/Designate. Gandhi was interned at the Aga Khan Palace in Pune along with his wife Kasturba Gandhi and secretary Mahadev Desai.
Why others are wrong: Cellular Jail was for radical revolutionary nationalists; Yerwada Jail was where Gandhi was held during the 1930-32 Civil Disobedience phase; Naini Prison housed leaders like Nehru at various intervals but not Gandhi in 1942.

19. **Which British institutional mission arrived in India in 1942, whose failure directly triggered the launch of the Quit India Movement?**
   - A) Simon Commission
   - B) Cripps Mission
   - C) Cabinet Mission
   - D) Wavell Plan
   - **Answer: B**
   - Explanation: The Cripps Mission, led by Sir Stafford Cripps, came to India in March 1942 to secure full Indian cooperation for WWII in exchange for post-war dominion status. The proposals were rejected by all Indian factions, leading Gandhi to call it a "post-dated cheque on a crashing bank," which paved the way for the Quit India struggle.
Why others are wrong: The Simon Commission arrived in 1928; the Cabinet Mission came later in 1946 to negotiate the transfer of power; the Wavell Plan was proposed at the Shimla Conference in 1945.

20. **Who among the following played a heroic role in running an underground secret radio station to broadcast news during the early phase of the Quit India Movement?**
   - A) Sarojini Naidu
   - B) Usha Mehta
   - C) Aruna Asaf Ali
   - D) Sucheta Kripalani
   - **Answer: B**
   - Explanation: Usha Mehta, a young activist, helped establish and operate the "Secret Congress Radio," an underground broadcasting station that kept the public informed about the movement's progress after mainstream leaders were jailed.
Why others are wrong: Sarojini Naidu was arrested along with Gandhi; Aruna Asaf Ali is famous for hoisting the Congress flag at Gowalia Tank Maidan; Sucheta Kripalani worked extensively in underground coordination but was not the primary coordinator of the radio setup.

21. **Which major publication was started by Mahatma Gandhi in 1933 to propagate his campaign against untouchability and uplift the depressed classes?**
   - A) Young India
   - B) Harijan
   - C) Indian Opinion
   - D) Kesari
   - **Answer: B**
   - Explanation: In 1933, Gandhi started the weekly journal 'Harijan' (meaning "Children of God"), a term he popularized for the untouchables, to advocate for social justice, structural reforms, and the eradication of untouchability.
Why others are wrong: 'Young India' was an earlier English weekly edited by Gandhi from 1919 to 1931; 'Indian Opinion' was started by him during his struggle in South Africa; 'Kesari' was a Marathi newspaper founded by Bal Gangadhar Tilak.

22. **What was the primary objective of the Individual Satyagraha launched by Mahatma Gandhi in October 1940?**
   - A) To demand immediate violent overthrow of the British administration
   - B) To assert the right to free speech against drawing India into World War II without its consent
   - C) To protest against the partition proposals of the Muslim League
   - D) To completely paralyze the railway network across North India
   - **Answer: B**
   - Explanation: Individual Satyagraha was launched to quietly but firmly assert the right to freedom of speech to protest against Indiaâ€™s forced inclusion in World War II without consulting its people, choosing selected individuals rather than mass agitations to avoid disrupting the war effort excessively.
Why others are wrong: The movement was explicitly non-violent; it predated the final institutionalized partition debates; it avoided mass disruptions like blocking railways.

23. **Who was selected by Mahatma Gandhi as the very first Individual Satyagrahi in 1940?**
   - A) Jawaharlal Nehru
   - B) Acharya Vinoba Bhave
   - C) Brahma Datt
   - D) Sardar Vallabhbhai Patel
   - **Answer: B**
   - Explanation: Acharya Vinoba Bhave was chosen by Gandhi as the first satyagrahi to offer individual resistance, followed by Jawaharlal Nehru as the second.
Why others are wrong: Jawaharlal Nehru was the second satyagrahi; Brahma Datt was the third; Sardar Patel was a crucial organizer but did not initiate the sequence.

24. **In which South African city was Mahatma Gandhi famously thrown off a train in 1893, an event that catalyzed his lifelong fight against racial discrimination?**
   - A) Johannesburg
   - B) Pretoria
   - C) Pietermaritzburg
   - D) Durban
   - **Answer: C**
   - Explanation: On June 7, 1893, while travelling to Pretoria, Gandhi was forcibly removed from a first-class compartment at the Pietermaritzburg railway station because of his race, an incident that transformed his political outlook.
Why others are wrong: Johannesburg and Durban were key centers of his legal practice and ashrams, and Pretoria was his destination, but the actual train incident occurred at Pietermaritzburg.

25. **Which philosophical text had the most profound impact on Gandhiâ€™s concept of Satyagraha and selfless duty, frequently referred to by him as his "spiritual dictionary"?**
   - A) The Bhagavad Gita
   - B) Unto This Last by John Ruskin
   - C) The Kingdom of God Is Within You by Leo Tolstoy
   - D) The Social Contract by Rousseau
   - **Answer: A**
   - Explanation: Mahatma Gandhi regarded the Bhagavad Gita as his eternal guide and spiritual dictionary, deeply drawing his principles of Ahimsa, Satya, and Nishkama Karma from its verses.
Why others are wrong: 'Unto This Last' inspired his economic views on Sarvodaya and bread labor; Tolstoy's work solidified his non-violent philosophy; Rousseau's text influenced general democratic thought but wasn't Gandhi's personal spiritual guide.

26. **The historic Lahore Session of the Indian National Congress in 1929, which passed the Purna Swaraj resolution, was presided over by which leader?**
   - A) Motilal Nehru
   - B) Jawaharlal Nehru
   - C) Mahatma Gandhi
   - D) Madan Mohan Malaviya
   - **Answer: B**
   - Explanation: Jawaharlal Nehru presided over the Lahore Session in December 1929, where the Congress unfurled the tricolor flag and passed the historic resolution demanding Purna Swaraj or Complete Independence.
Why others are wrong: Motilal Nehru presided over the 1928 Calcutta Session; Mahatma Gandhi presided in 1924; Madan Mohan Malaviya presided over other sessions but not the 1929 landmark event.

27. **What date was declared as the first "Independence Day" to be celebrated across India during the Lahore Congress of 1929?**
   - A) August 15, 1930
   - B) January 26, 1930
   - C) October 2, 1930
   - D) April 6, 1930
   - **Answer: B**
   - Explanation: The Congress issued a pledge asking the public to celebrate January 26, 1930, as India's Independence Day, a date that later inspired the choice for Republic Day in 1950.
Why others are wrong: August 15 became the actual Independence Day in 1947; October 2 is Gandhiâ€™s birthday; April 6, 1930, was the day Gandhi broke the salt law at Dandi.

28. **The Rowlatt Act of 1919, which triggered massive protests and the subsequent Jallianwala Bagh tragedy, allowed the British government to execute which measure?**
   - A) Confiscate all vernacular printing presses without warning
   - B) Detain any person without trial for up to two years on suspicion of treason
   - C) Ban the domestic entry of any Indian into civil administrative services
   - D) Enforce compulsory military enlistment for all adult males
   - **Answer: B**
   - Explanation: The Anarchical and Revolutionary Crimes Act of 1919 (Rowlatt Act) authorized the government to imprison any person suspected of terrorism or sedition for up to two years without a trial, severely undermining civil liberties.
Why others are wrong: Confiscating presses was part of the Vernacular Press Act of 1878; it did not bar administrative entries or enforce compulsory military conscription.

29. **During the Khilafat Movement, which collaborated closely with Gandhi's Non-Cooperation Movement, who were the primary leaders known as the 'Ali Brothers'?**
   - A) Muhammad Ali Jinnah and Shaukat Ali
   - B) Shaukat Ali and Mohammad Ali Jauhar
   - C) Asaf Ali and Aruna Asaf Ali
   - D) Zakir Husain and Abul Kalam Azad
   - **Answer: B**
   - Explanation: The Khilafat Movement in India was led by the Ali brothersâ€”Shaukat Ali and Mohammad Ali Jauharâ€”along with other leaders, protesting against the dismantling of the Ottoman Caliphate.
Why others are wrong: Muhammad Ali Jinnah strongly opposed the mixture of religion and mass politics during the Khilafat phase; Asaf Ali was a separate prominent lawyer and diplomat; Zakir Husain and Azad were key nationalist leaders but not the 'Ali Brothers'.

30. **Which fundamental pact or declaration during the Gandhian era first formally accepted the demand for a constituent assembly to frame an independent constitution?**
   - A) August Offer 1940
   - B) Cripps Proposal 1942
   - C) Cabinet Mission Plan 1946
   - D) Nehru Report 1928
   - **Answer: A**
   - Explanation: The demand for a Constituent Assembly was first officially conceded by the British Government, though with conditions, in the 'August Offer' of 1940 issued by Viceroy Linlithgow.
Why others are wrong: The Cripps Proposal and Cabinet Mission expanded significantly on the mechanism, but the initial formal acceptance happened in 1940; the Nehru Report was an indigenous draft constitution, not a British concession.

## Modern - Acts & Reforms (Regulating Act, Pitt's India Act, Charter Acts, Government of India Acts) (Modern)

1.  Topic Introduction
    The given questions focus on Indian Polity and Modern Indian History, specifically covering the Constitutional Development of India and Historical Background of the Indian Constitution. This encompasses the various legislative acts passed by the British Parliament between 1773 and 1947 to govern British India. These regulations laid the structural foundation for the administrative, legislative, and judicial framework of modern India, making it a highly crucial and mandatory core segment of the General Awareness syllabus for the SSC CGL exam.

2.  Core Concepts Explained Simply
    
      - Company Rule (1773â€“1858): The period when the East India Company ruled India under the oversight of the British Parliament via acts like the Regulating Act and Charter Acts, focusing heavily on trade monopolies and gradual administrative centralization.
      - Crown Rule (1858â€“1947): Initiated after the 1857 revolt, where direct sovereignty was transferred to the British Crown, marked by gradual legislative devolution, the portfolio system, and the introduction of representative elements.
      - Centralization vs. Devolution: Centralization reached its peak under the Charter Act of 1833 by creating a singular pan-India executive, while subsequent Council Acts gradually devolved powers back to provinces, culminating in provincial autonomy in 1935.
      - Communal Electorates: A system introduced by the British where seats in the legislature were reserved exclusively for specific religious communities, and only members of that community could vote for their respective candidates.

3.  Important Facts and Dates
    
      - Regulating Act 1773: Made Governors of Bombay and Madras subordinate to Bengal; established the Supreme Court at Calcutta (1774).
      - Pitt's India Act 1784: Established the dual system of governance through the Board of Control (political) and Court of Directors (commercial).
      - Charter Act 1813: Ended the Company's trade monopoly except for tea and China; allocated Rs. 1 Lakh for education.
      - Charter Act 1833: Made the Governor-General of Bengal the Governor-General of India (Lord William Bentinck); completely ended all commercial operations of the EIC.
      - Charter Act 1853: Separated legislative and executive wings of the Governor-General's Council; introduced an open competitive exam for Civil Services.
      - Government of India Act 1858: Transferred power to the British Crown; created the office of the Secretary of State for India.
      - Indian Councils Act 1861: Institutionalized the portfolio system; gave ordinance-making powers to the Viceroy.
      - Indian Councils Act 1892: Introduced the concept of indirect elections; permitted councils to discuss the annual budget.
      - Indian Councils Act 1909 (Morley-Minto): Introduced separate electorates for Muslims; Satyendra Prasad Sinha became the first Indian in the executive council.
      - Government of India Act 1919 (Montagu-Chelmsford): Introduced provincial diarchy (Reserved vs. Transferred) and central bicameralism; set up the Public Service Commission (1926).
      - Government of India Act 1935: Envisioned an All-India Federation; abolished provincial diarchy to introduce provincial autonomy; established the Federal Court (1937); separated Burma.

4.  Related Concepts Frequently Asked in SSC Exams
    
      - Governor-General and Viceroy Titles: SSC regularly tests the first and last figures of these titles (e.g., First GG of Bengal = Warren Hastings; First GG of India = William Bentinck; First Viceroy = Lord Canning).
      - Emergency Ordinances: The six-month validity rule of the Viceroy's emergency ordinance introduced in 1861 mirrors the modern President's ordinance powers under Article 123.
      - Legislative Lists: The distribution of legislative powers into Federal, Provincial, and Concurrent lists under the 1935 Act served as the structural precursor to the Seventh Schedule of the Indian Constitution.
      - Constitutional Commissions: The statutory ten-year review clause embedded in the 1919 Act is heavily queried due to its direct link with the arrival of the Simon Commission in 1927.

5.  Differences Between Similar Terms
    
      - Board of Control vs. Court of Directors: The Board of Control was a 6-member British government body overseeing political, military, and civil affairs, while the Court of Directors represented the company's shareholders managing purely commercial interests.
      - Diarchy vs. Bicameralism: Diarchy refers to a dual rule or division of executive subjects into two categories (Transferred and Reserved), whereas Bicameralism refers to a two-house legislative structure (Upper House and Lower House).
      - Provincial Diarchy vs. Federal Diarchy: The 1919 Act introduced diarchy strictly within the provinces. The 1935 Act completely abolished provincial diarchy to grant them autonomy, while simultaneously proposing diarchy at the central/federal level.

6.  Examples
    
      - Indirect Election Element: The Indian Councils Act 1892 allowed bodies like universities and district boards to recommend non-official members, serving as the earliest practical framework of representation without using the term 'election'.
      - Portfolio System Implementation: Lord Canning distributed specific government departments to individual council members in 1859, a template formalized in 1861 that matches the modern cabinet ministry system.
      - Residuary Power Allocation: In the 1935 framework, instead of assigning leftover powers to the center or provinces, they were uniquely given as personal discretionary veto powers to the Governor-General.

7.  Previous-Year Exam Perspective
    
      - SSC CGL exams consistently isolate specific provisions and map them directly to their corresponding historical acts. Questions frequently target the act that introduced separate electorates (1909), the act that ended the company's trade monopoly entirely (1833), and the year the Central Public Service Commission was established (1926). Additionally, candidates are regularly tested on the exact acts responsible for establishing the Supreme Court at Calcutta (1773) versus the Federal Court at Delhi (1935).

8.  Quick Revision Points and Mnemonics
    
      - Supreme Court Foundations: 1773 Act = Calcutta Supreme Court (1774).
      - Dual Control: Pitt's India Act 1784 = Dual Government (Board of Control + Court of Directors).
      - Total Trade Abolition: 1833 = 33 ends EIC commercial free-for-all (complete trade monopoly end).
      - Civil Service Exams: 1853 = Open competition starts in practice (Macaulay Committee 1854).
      - Cabinet/Portfolio Birth: 1861 Act = Portfolio system institutionalized.
      - Budget Discussion: 1892 Act = Speak on budget, but no voting power.
      - Father of Communal Electorates: Lord Minto (1909 Act).
      - Double Diarchy Clue: 1919 Act = Diarchy at Provinces; 1935 Act = Autonomy to Provinces, Diarchy to Center.
      - Apex Court Transition: 1935 Act = Federal Court of India (1937).

### Quick Practice (30 MCQs)

1. **Which act first introduced the element of election in British India, though the word 'election' was not explicitly used in the text?**
   - A) Regulating Act 1773
   - B) Indian Councils Act 1892
   - C) Pitt's India Act 1784
   - D) Charter Act 1833
   - **Answer: B**
   - Explanation: The Indian Councils Act 1892 introduced the principle of representation and allowed for some non-official members to be nominated to councils based on recommendations from bodies like district boards and universities, effectively introducing the concept of an indirect election.
Why others are wrong: The Regulating Act 1773 centralized control in Bengal; Pitt's India Act established the dual system of control; the Charter Act 1833 centralized the administration completely and made the Governor-General of Bengal the Governor-General of India.

2. **Which act created the 'Board of Control' to manage political affairs while leaving commercial operations to the Court of Directors?**
   - A) Regulating Act 1773
   - B) Pitt's India Act 1784
   - C) Charter Act 1813
   - D) Government of India Act 1858
   - **Answer: B**
   - Explanation: Pitt's India Act of 1784 established a system of dual government by creating the Board of Control to oversee political, civil, and military affairs, separating it from the commercial affairs managed by the Court of Directors.
Why others are wrong: The Regulating Act 1773 did not separate political and commercial controls systematically; the Charter Act 1813 ended the trade monopoly except for tea and China; the Government of India Act 1858 abolished the Board of Control and Court of Directors entirely.

3. **By which act was the trade monopoly of the East India Company completely abolished, including trade with China and trade in tea?**
   - A) Charter Act 1793
   - B) Charter Act 1813
   - C) Charter Act 1833
   - D) Charter Act 1853
   - **Answer: C**
   - Explanation: The Charter Act of 1833 ended the East India Company's status as a commercial body, making it a purely administrative body, thereby completely ending its trade monopoly including tea and trade with China.
Why others are wrong: The Charter Act 1793 renewed the charter for 20 years without major trade changes; the Charter Act 1813 abolished the monopoly for general trade but explicitly preserved monopolies on tea and trade with China; the Charter Act 1853 focused on administrative reforms and legislative separations.

4. **Which act designated the Governor-General of Bengal as the Governor-General of India and vested in him all civil and military power?**
   - A) Pitt's India Act 1784
   - B) Charter Act 1813
   - C) Charter Act 1833
   - D) Government of India Act 1858
   - **Answer: C**
   - Explanation: The Charter Act of 1833 was the final step towards centralization in British India, creating the post of Governor-General of India, with Lord William Bentinck becoming the first.
Why others are wrong: Pitt's India Act 1784 altered council structures but didn't redefine the pan-India post; the Charter Act 1813 focused on trade and education; the Government of India Act 1858 replaced the Governor-General with the title of Viceroy under the British Crown.

5. **Which act introduced an open competition system for the selection and recruitment of civil servants for the first time in practice?**
   - A) Charter Act 1833
   - B) Charter Act 1853
   - C) Indian Councils Act 1861
   - D) Indian Councils Act 1892
   - **Answer: B**
   - Explanation: The Charter Act of 1853 introduced an open competitive system for civil services selection, leading to the appointment of the Macaulay Committee on Indian Civil Service in 1854.
Why others are wrong: The Charter Act of 1833 attempted to introduce an open competition but the provision was negated due to opposition from the Court of Directors; the Indian Councils Acts of 1861 and 1892 focused on expanding legislative councils rather than initiating the civil service exam system.

6. **The system of 'Diarchy' was introduced at the provincial level by which of the following acts?**
   - A) Indian Councils Act 1909
   - B) Government of India Act 1919
   - C) Government of India Act 1935
   - D) Indian Independence Act 1947
   - **Answer: B**
   - Explanation: The Government of India Act 1919 (Montagu-Chelmsford Reforms) introduced Diarchy at the provincial level, dividing provincial subjects into 'Transferred' and 'Reserved' categories.
Why others are wrong: The Indian Councils Act 1909 introduced separate electorates; the Government of India Act 1935 abolished provincial diarchy and proposed it at the federal level; the Indian Independence Act 1947 terminated British rule.

7. **Which act laid the foundation of central administration in India by subordinating the Governors of Bombay and Madras to the Governor-General of Bengal?**
   - A) Regulating Act 1773
   - B) Pitt's India Act 1784
   - C) Charter Act 1793
   - D) Charter Act 1813
   - **Answer: A**
   - Explanation: The Regulating Act of 1773 was the first step by the British government to control and regulate the East India Company's affairs, making the presidencies of Bombay and Madras subordinate to Bengal.
Why others are wrong: Pitt's India Act 1784 consolidated political oversight via London; the Charter Acts of 1793 and 1813 extended existing frameworks without initiating this initial subordination.

8. **Under which act was a Supreme Court established at Fort William in Calcutta consisting of a Chief Justice and three other judges?**
   - A) Regulating Act 1773
   - B) Pitt's India Act 1784
   - C) Charter Act 1833
   - D) Indian High Courts Act 1861
   - **Answer: A**
   - Explanation: The Regulating Act of 1773 provided for the establishment of a Supreme Court at Calcutta in 1774, with Sir Elijah Impey serving as the first Chief Justice.
Why others are wrong: Pitt's India Act 1784 did not deal with judicial restructuring; the Charter Act 1833 altered legislative power; the Indian High Courts Act 1861 amalgamated existing courts into High Courts much later.

9. **Which act allowed Christian missionaries to come to India for the purpose of religious and moral improvement?**
   - A) Charter Act 1793
   - B) Charter Act 1813
   - C) Charter Act 1833
   - D) Charter Act 1853
   - **Answer: B**
   - Explanation: The Charter Act of 1813 permitted Christian missionaries to enter British India to propagate their religion and spread western education.
Why others are wrong: The Charter Act 1793 did not lift restrictions on missionaries; the Charter Acts of 1833 and 1853 expanded other administrative rights but the legal baseline was established in 1813.

10. **Which act first separated the legislative and executive functions of the Governor-Generalâ€™s council, creating a separate 6-member Legislative Council?**
   - A) Charter Act 1833
   - B) Charter Act 1853
   - C) Indian Councils Act 1861
   - D) Indian Councils Act 1909
   - **Answer: B**
   - Explanation: The Charter Act of 1853 marked the beginning of a parliamentary system for India by separating the legislative wing from the executive wing of the Governor-General's Council.
Why others are wrong: The Charter Act 1833 added a law member only for consultation; the Indian Councils Act 1861 expanded legislative representation but didn't create the initial separation; the Act of 1909 expanded council sizing.

11. **The rule of the East India Company was terminated, and the governance of India was transferred directly to the British Crown by which act?**
   - A) Charter Act 1853
   - B) Government of India Act 1858
   - C) Indian Councils Act 1861
   - D) Queenâ€™s Proclamation 1877
   - **Answer: B**
   - Explanation: Enacted in the wake of the Revolt of 1857, the Government of India Act 1858 transferred the sovereignty over India from the East India Company to the British Crown.
Why others are wrong: The Charter Act 1853 renewed company rule indefinitely but didn't end it; the Indian Councils Act 1861 adjusted council frameworks under the Crown; the Queen's Proclamation occurred later as a formal event.

12. **Which act introduced the portfolio system, under which a member of the Governor-General's council was made in charge of one or more government departments?**
   - A) Government of India Act 1858
   - B) Indian Councils Act 1861
   - C) Indian Councils Act 1892
   - D) Indian Councils Act 1909
   - **Answer: B**
   - Explanation: The Indian Councils Act of 1861 institutionalized the portfolio system introduced by Lord Canning in 1859, laying the foundations of cabinet governance.
Why others are wrong: The Government of India Act 1858 shifted structural sovereignty; the Indian Councils Acts of 1892 and 1909 expanded representation and powers but did not introduce the core departmental portfolio concept.

13. **Which act is notoriously known for introducing communal electorates in India by granting separate electorates to Muslims?**
   - A) Indian Councils Act 1892
   - B) Indian Councils Act 1909
   - C) Government of India Act 1919
   - D) Government of India Act 1935
   - **Answer: B**
   - Explanation: The Indian Councils Act 1909, also known as the Morley-Minto Reforms, legally recognized communalism by providing separate electorates for Muslims.
Why others are wrong: The Indian Councils Act 1892 didn't mention communal electorates; the Government of India Act 1919 extended separate electorates to Sikhs, Indian Christians, Anglo-Indians, and Europeans; the Act of 1935 extended them to depressed classes, women, and labor.

14. **Who was the first Indian to join the Governor-Generalâ€™s Executive Council as a law member under the provisions of the Indian Councils Act 1909?**
   - A) Satyendra Prasad Sinha
   - B) W.C. Bonnerjee
   - C) Dadabhai Naoroji
   - D) Tej Bahadur Sapru
   - **Answer: A**
   - Explanation: Satyendra Prasad Sinha became the first Indian to enter the Viceroy's Executive Council as a law member following the Morley-Minto reforms.
Why others are wrong: W.C. Bonnerjee was the first INC president; Dadabhai Naoroji was a British MP; Tej Bahadur Sapru joined the council much later in the 1920s.

15. **Bicameralism at the national level (Central Legislature) was introduced for the first time by which act?**
   - A) Indian Councils Act 1909
   - B) Government of India Act 1919
   - C) Government of India Act 1935
   - D) Indian Independence Act 1947
   - **Answer: B**
   - Explanation: The Government of India Act 1919 replaced the imperial legislative council with a bicameral legislature consisting of a Council of State (Upper House) and a Legislative Assembly (Lower House).
Why others are wrong: The Act of 1909 kept the center unicameral; the Act of 1935 introduced bicameralism in provinces while retaining/altering the center's design; the 1947 act made the Constituent Assemblies sovereign.

16. **The Government of India Act 1919 provided for the establishment of a Public Service Commission, which was subsequently set up in which year?**
   - A) 1921
   - B) 1926
   - C) 1935
   - D) 1947
   - **Answer: B**
   - Explanation: Following the recommendations of the Lee Commission, a Central Public Service Commission was set up in 1926 to recruit civil servants.
Why others are wrong: 1921 was when the act came into force; 1935 was the year the Federal Public Service Commission was designed under a new act; 1947 was the year of independence.

17. **Which act provided for the establishment of an All-India Federation consisting of provinces and princely states as units, though it never came into effect?**
   - A) Government of India Act 1919
   - B) Government of India Act 1935
   - C) Indian Independence Act 1947
   - D) Constitution of India 1950
   - **Answer: B**
   - Explanation: The Government of India Act 1935 envisioned an All-India Federation combining British Indian provinces and princely states, but it failed to materialize because the princely states refused to join.
Why others are wrong: The 1919 Act kept a unitary framework with devloved powers; the 1947 Act partitioned the territory; the 1950 Constitution created a Union of States successfully.

18. **By which act was the system of provincial diarchy abolished and provincial autonomy introduced in its place?**
   - A) Indian Councils Act 1909
   - B) Government of India Act 1919
   - C) Government of India Act 1935
   - D) Indian Independence Act 1947
   - **Answer: C**
   - Explanation: The Government of India Act 1935 abolished diarchy in the provinces and established provincial autonomy, making the provincial executive responsible to the elected legislature.
Why others are wrong: The Act of 1909 did not feature diarchy; the Act of 1919 introduced provincial diarchy; the Act of 1947 gave independent dominion status.

19. **The Federal Court of India, which was later converted into the Supreme Court of India, was established under the provisions of which act?**
   - A) Indian High Courts Act 1861
   - B) Government of India Act 1919
   - C) Government of India Act 1935
   - D) Indian Independence Act 1947
   - **Answer: C**
   - Explanation: The Government of India Act 1935 provided for the setting up of a Federal Court, which was established in Delhi in 1937.
Why others are wrong: The High Courts Act 1861 created regional High Courts; the 1919 Act did not alter the supreme judicial apex; the 1947 Act focused on political sovereignty transfer.

20. **Which act separated Burma (now Myanmar) from British India?**
   - A) Government of India Act 1919
   - B) Government of India Act 1935
   - C) Indian Independence Act 1947
   - D) Cabinet Mission Plan 1946
   - **Answer: B**
   - Explanation: Based on provisions within the Government of India Act 1935, Burma was formally separated from the administration of British India in 1937.
Why others are wrong: The 1919 Act left Burma as a province of India; the 1947 Act dealt exclusively with the partition of India and Pakistan; the Cabinet Mission Plan focused on the constituent assembly.

21. **Which act explicitly allocated a sum of one lakh rupees annually for the spread of education and promotion of science in India?**
   - A) Regulating Act 1773
   - B) Charter Act 1813
   - C) Charter Act 1833
   - D) Charter Act 1853
   - **Answer: B**
   - Explanation: The Charter Act of 1813 contained a clause stating that a sum of not less than one lakh rupees each year would be set apart for the revival and improvement of literature.
Why others are wrong: The 1773 Act had no education funding; the 1833 and 1853 acts built upon subsequent educational policies (like Macaulay's minutes) but did not initiate this initial one lakh allocation.

22. **Which act changed the designation of the office of the Secretary of State for India to handle relations via the Commonwealth, preparing for independence?**
   - A) Government of India Act 1919
   - B) Government of India Act 1935
   - C) Indian Independence Act 1947
   - D) Cabinet Mission Plan 1946
   - **Answer: C**
   - Explanation: The Indian Independence Act of 1947 abolished the office of the Secretary of State for India and transferred its functions to the Secretary of State for Commonwealth Relations.
Why others are wrong: The 1919 and 1935 acts sustained the office of the Secretary of State for India as a core pillar of imperial governance over the colony.

23. **The residency powers under the three legislative lists (Federal, Provincial, and Concurrent) in the Government of India Act 1935 were given to whom?**
   - A) The Federal Legislature
   - B) The Provincial Legislatures
   - C) The Governor-General
   - D) The British Parliament
   - **Answer: C**
   - Explanation: Uniquely, the Government of India Act 1935 vested the residuary legislative powers neither in the federal nor provincial legislatures, but directly with the Governor-General.
Why others are wrong: The Federal and Provincial legislatures had exclusive domains; the British Parliament retained constituent power but did not exercise day-to-day residuary powers.

24. **Which act authorized the Governor-General to issue ordinances during an emergency without the concurrence of the legislative council?**
   - A) Pitt's India Act 1784
   - B) Charter Act 1853
   - C) Indian Councils Act 1861
   - D) Indian Councils Act 1892
   - **Answer: C**
   - Explanation: The Indian Councils Act 1861 empowered the Viceroy to issue ordinances, valid for six months, during times of emergency without council approval.
Why others are wrong: The 1784 and 1853 acts did not define an emergency executive ordinance framework; the 1892 Act expanded discussions on budgets but kept executive emergency rules unchanged.

25. **Which act designated the first Law Member to the Governor-Generalâ€™s Council, a post initially occupied by Lord Macaulay?**
   - A) Charter Act 1813
   - B) Charter Act 1833
   - C) Charter Act 1853
   - D) Government of India Act 1858
   - **Answer: B**
   - Explanation: The Charter Act of 1833 added a fourth member to the Governor-General's executive council specifically as a Law Member to assist in codifying laws.
Why others are wrong: The 1813 Act made no additions to council staffing; the 1853 Act made the law member a full member; the 1858 Act transformed the governance structure entirely.

26. **The introduction of local representation in the Central Legislative Council was a key feature of which act?**
   - A) Charter Act 1833
   - B) Charter Act 1853
   - C) Indian Councils Act 1861
   - D) Indian Councils Act 1892
   - **Answer: B**
   - Explanation: The Charter Act of 1853 introduced local representation in the Indian Central Legislative Council, requiring four out of six new legislative members to be appointed by the local governments of Madras, Bombay, Bengal, and Agra.
Why others are wrong: The 1833 Act centralized all laws under one body with no local representatives; the 1861 and 1892 acts added non-official Indian members but the structural start of designated provincial government representation occurred in 1853.

27. **Which act allowed members of the legislative council to discuss the annual budget and ask questions on public interest, though they could not vote on it?**
   - A) Indian Councils Act 1861
   - B) Indian Councils Act 1892
   - C) Indian Councils Act 1909
   - D) Government of India Act 1919
   - **Answer: B**
   - Explanation: The Indian Councils Act 1892 expanded the functions of the legislative councils by giving them the power of discussing the budget and addressing questions to the executive.
Why others are wrong: The 1861 Act explicitly restricted councils from discussing anything other than the specific legislative bills before them; the 1909 Act moved further by allowing supplementary questions and voting on resolutions; the 1919 Act introduced partial voting on budget demands.

28. **Under the Government of India Act 1919, which of the following subjects was classified as a 'Reserved' subject under the provincial diarchy?**
   - A) Education
   - B) Local Self-Government
   - C) Public Health
   - D) Land Revenue and Police
   - **Answer: D**
   - Explanation: Under the 1919 Act's diarchy system, crucial subjects like Land Revenue, Justice, Police, and Finance were 'Reserved' and administered by the Governor and his executive council without responsibility to the legislature.
Why others are wrong: Education, Local Self-Government, and Public Health were classified as 'Transferred' subjects administered by the Governor acting with elected ministers.

29. **Which act officially abolished the East India Company's Court of Directors and the Board of Control, replacing them with a Secretary of State for India and a 15-member Council of India?**
   - A) Charter Act 1853
   - B) Government of India Act 1858
   - C) Indian Councils Act 1861
   - D) Queen's Proclamation 1858
   - **Answer: B**
   - Explanation: The Government of India Act 1858 streamlined home governance by ending the dual control system of the Company (Board of Control and Court of Directors) and transferring their cumulative powers to the Secretary of State for India.
Why others are wrong: The 1853 Act kept the Court of Directors active with reduced numbers; the 1861 Act dealt with councils in India rather than the home government in London; the Queen's Proclamation was a political declaration rather than the statute that executed the structural abolition.

30. **By which act was the statutory declaration made that the British Crown would appoint a commission after 10 years to look into the working of the constitutional system in India (which led to the Simon Commission)?**
   - A) Indian Councils Act 1909
   - B) Government of India Act 1919
   - C) Government of India Act 1935
   - D) Indian Independence Act 1947
   - **Answer: B**
   - Explanation: The Government of India Act 1919 included a provision stating that a statutory commission would be appointed ten years after the passing of the act to report on its operations, leading to the appointment of the Simon Commission in late 1927.
Why others are wrong: The 1909 Act had no built-in automatic sunset review commission timeline; the 1935 Act was replaced directly by the war and partition processes; the 1947 Act served as the final operational transfer of complete independence.

## Modern - Administration (Governor Generals, Viceroys, Policies, Committees) (Modern)

1.  Topic Introduction
    The given questions focus on Modern Indian History, specifically covering the British Administration in India through Governor-Generals, Viceroys, administrative policies, acts, and commissions. This encompasses the institutional framework established from the Regulating Act of 1773 under the East India Company to the final phases of British rule under the Crown. Mastering these administrative shifts, educational plans, land revenue policies, and legal frameworks is critical for securing high scores in the General Awareness history section of the SSC CGL exam.

2.  Core Concepts Explained Simply  - Governor-General vs. Viceroy: The Regulating Act of 1773 created the title of Governor-General of Bengal, which was upgraded to Governor-General of India in 1833. Following the 1857 revolt, the Government of India Act 1858 replaced the Company rule with the British Crown, changing the designation to Viceroy (the monarch's direct representative).
  - Imperial Administrative Policies: Mechanisms used by the British to annex territories and consolidate power. Prominent examples include the Subsidiary Alliance (subjugating princely states by placing British troops in their territory) and the Doctrine of Lapse (annexing princely states lacking a direct legal male heir).
  - Land Revenue Systems: Structural methods implemented to collect land taxes, such as the Permanent Settlement, which fixed revenue permanently and recognized Zamindars as landlords, creating a loyal class of intermediaries.
  - Commissions and Education Plans: Special committees appointed to investigate institutional frameworks or resolve crises, covering areas such as education (Wood's Despatch, Hunter Commission, Sadler Commission, Sargent Plan), famines (MacDonnell Committee), civil service quotas (Lee Commission), and constitutional reforms (Simon Commission, Muddiman Committee).3.  Important Facts and Dates  - 1773: Regulating Act passes; Warren Hastings becomes the first Governor-General of Bengal.
  - 1793: Lord Cornwallis introduces the Permanent Settlement system.
  - 1829: Lord William Bentinck formally abolishes the practice of Sati via Bengal Regulation XVII.
  - 1854: Woodâ€™s Despatch is issued during Lord Dalhousie's tenure.
  - 1858: Government of India Act passed; Lord Canning becomes the first Viceroy of India.
  - 1878: Lord Lytton passes the repressive Vernacular Press Act to suppress Indian language newspapers.
  - 1882: Lord Ripon issues the local self-government resolution and establishes the Hunter Commission on education.
  - 1905: Lord Curzon orchestrates the controversial Partition of Bengal.
  - 1909: Morley-Minto Reforms introduce separate electorates for Muslims under Lord Minto II.
  - 1911: King George V hosts the Delhi Durbar under Lord Hardinge II, shifting the capital from Calcutta to Delhi.
  - 1919: Rowlatt Act passed and Jallianwala Bagh massacre occurs under Lord Chelmsford.4.  Related Concepts Frequently Asked in SSC Exams  - Land Tenure Variations: SSC frequently tests the founders and regions of alternative systems. The Ryotwari System was introduced by Thomas Munro and Alexander Read in Madras/Bombay, while the Mahalwari System was designed by Holt Mackenzie in the North-West Province.
  - Act Names and Proponents: Constitutional acts are often double-named after the Secretary of State and the Viceroy. For example, the Morley-Minto Reforms (1909) and the Montagu-Chelmsford Reforms (1919).
  - Social Reform Regulations: Beyond Sati, SSC frequently asks about the Hindu Widows' Remarriage Act (1856), passed during the tenure of Lord Dalhousie and executed under Lord Canning.
  - Post-War Developmental Blueprints: Infrastructure plans from the 1940s are highly tested, such as the Nagpur Plan for road and highway development.5.  Differences Between Similar Terms  - Subsidiary Alliance vs. Doctrine of Lapse: The Subsidiary Alliance (Wellesley) targeted military independence by forcing rulers to pay for British military protection, whereas the Doctrine of Lapse (Dalhousie) targeted political lineage by directly annexing states without natural male heirs.
  - Governor-General of Bengal vs. Governor-General of India: The Governor-General of Bengal (started with Warren Hastings in 1773) held authority primarily over Bengal with oversight of Madras and Bombay. The Governor-General of India (started with William Bentinck in 1833 via the Charter Act) held complete legislative power over all British territories in India.
  - Woodâ€™s Despatch vs. Hunter Commission: Woodâ€™s Despatch (1854) established the foundational roadmap for a complete multi-tiered education network from primary schools to universities. The Hunter Commission (1882) specifically evaluated the progress made since 1854, recommending that the government leave higher education to private enterprise and prioritize local primary and secondary education.6.  Examples  - Application of Annexation Policies: Satara, Jhansi, and Nagpur serve as classic textbook examples of territories directly absorbed into British India under Dalhousie's Doctrine of Lapse.
  - Political Assassinations: The murder of Lord Mayo in 1872 by Sher Ali Afridi in the Andaman Islands stands as the singular instance of a sitting Viceroy being assassinated in office.
  - Separation of Budgets: The Acworth Committee recommendation led to the historic decoupling of the Railway Budget from the General Budget in 1924, a structural economic convention that lasted until its merger back in 2017.7.  Previous-Year Exam Perspective
    SSC CGL history questions consistently emphasize matching key Governor-Generals or Viceroys with their signature acts, policies, or committees. Expect direct questions matching specific social changes (like the abolition of Sati or local self-government resolutions) with the exact administrative head in power. Furthermore, questions targeting specific committees (such as the Lee Commission for civil services or the MacDonnell Commission for famines) frequently test the primary operational purpose or the under-lying crisis that prompted the appointment.

8.  Quick Revision Points and Mnemonics  - First of Bengal: 1773 = Warren Hastings (Hastings hurried to be first in Bengal).
  - First of India: 1833 = William Bentinck (Bentinck benefited the whole of India).
  - First Viceroy: 1858 = Lord Canning (Canning caught the crown after the 1857 revolt).
  - Land Lord Creator: Permanent Settlement = Lord Cornwallis (Cornwallis created the Zamindar wall).
  - Annexation Trick: Doctrine of Lapse = Lord Dalhousie (Dalhousie dealt a lapse to heirs).
  - Father of Local Self-Govt: Lord Ripon (Ripon brought reforms for the public).
  - Partition Orchestrator: Bengal Partition 1905 = Lord Curzon (Curzon cut Bengal).
  - Separate Electorate Creator: Father of Communal Electorate = Lord Minto II (Minto minted divisions).
  - Capital Shift: 1911 Capital Shift = Lord Hardinge II (Hardinge handled the hard shift to Delhi).
  - Railway Separation: Acworth Committee = Railway Budget (Acworth accelerated the engines).

### Quick Practice (30 MCQs)

1. **Who was the first Governor-General of Bengal appointed under the Regulating Act of 1773?**
   - A) Robert Clive
   - B) Warren Hastings
   - C) Lord Cornwallis
   - D) Lord William Bentinck
   - **Answer: B**
   - Explanation: The Regulating Act of 1773 elevated the Governor of Bengal, Warren Hastings, to the rank of Governor-General of Bengal, bringing Madras and Bombay presidencies under Bengal's control.
Why others are wrong: Robert Clive was the Governor of Bengal twice but never held the title of Governor-General; Lord Cornwallis introduced the Permanent Settlement later in 1793; Lord William Bentinck became the first Governor-General of India in 1833.

2. **Which British Governor-General introduced the system of 'Subsidiary Alliance' to establish British supremacy over Indian princely states?**
   - A) Lord Wellesley
   - B) Lord Dalhousie
   - C) Lord Canning
   - D) Lord Hastings
   - **Answer: A**
   - Explanation: Lord Wellesley (1798â€“1805) widely implemented the Subsidiary Alliance system, forcing Indian rulers to maintain British troops in their territory in exchange for protection, effectively stripping them of independence.
Why others are wrong: Lord Dalhousie is famous for the Doctrine of Lapse; Lord Canning was the first Viceroy after the 1857 revolt; Lord Hastings led the Third Anglo-Maratha War.

3. **The controversial 'Doctrine of Lapse', which led to the annexation of states like Satara, Jhansi, and Nagpur, was devised by whom?**
   - A) Lord Wellesley
   - B) Lord Dalhousie
   - C) Lord Curzon
   - D) Lord Irwin
   - **Answer: B**
   - Explanation: Lord Dalhousie institutionalized the Doctrine of Lapse, stating that any princely state under the direct or indirect influence of the British East India Company where the ruler did not have a legal male heir would be annexed.
Why others are wrong: Lord Wellesley introduced the Subsidiary Alliance; Lord Curzon is known for the Partition of Bengal; Lord Irwin signed the Gandhi-Irwin Pact in 1931.

4. **Who was the Governor-General of India when the Practice of Sati was formally abolished through a legal regulation in 1829?**
   - A) Lord William Bentinck
   - B) Lord Auckland
   - C) Lord Ellenborough
   - D) Lord Hardinge I
   - **Answer: A**
   - Explanation: Lord William Bentinck passed the Bengal Sati Regulation of 1829 with the strong backing of social reformer Raja Ram Mohan Roy, declaring the practice illegal and punishable by criminal courts.
Why others are wrong: Lord Auckland is known for the First Anglo-Afghan War; Lord Ellenborough annexed Sindh; Lord Hardinge I was involved in the First Anglo-Sikh War.

5. **Which administrative policy or system introduced by Lord Cornwallis in 1793 created a new class of landlords in Bengal, Bihar, and Odisha?**
   - A) Ryotwari Settlement
   - B) Mahalwari Settlement
   - C) Permanent Settlement
   - D) Dual Government System
   - **Answer: C**
   - Explanation: The Permanent Settlement (Zamindari System) fixed the land revenue collection rights permanently for landlords (Zamindars), making them the owners of the land as long as they paid the fixed revenue to the British.
Why others are wrong: Ryotwari was introduced in Madras by Thomas Munro; Mahalwari was introduced in North-West India by Holt Mackenzie; the Dual Government was introduced by Robert Clive after the Battle of Buxar.

6. **The Woodâ€™s Despatch of 1854, often referred to as the 'Magna Carta of English Education in India', was formulated during the tenure of which Governor-General?**
   - A) Lord Dalhousie
   - B) Lord Hardinge I
   - C) Lord Ellenborough
   - D) Lord Canning
   - **Answer: A**
   - Explanation: Charles Wood sent a dispatch to Governor-General Lord Dalhousie outlines a comprehensive plan for spreading education in India, leading to the creation of departments of public instruction and universities.
Why others are wrong: Lord Hardinge I promoted English education earlier but not the Despatch; Lord Ellenborough focused on defense and territorial issues; Lord Canning saw the actual execution of the universities during his term.

7. **Who served as the first Viceroy of India following the enactment of the Government of India Act 1858, which transferred power from the Company to the British Crown?**
   - A) Lord Dalhousie
   - B) Lord Canning
   - C) Lord Lawrence
   - D) Lord Mayo
   - **Answer: B**
   - Explanation: Lord Canning was the last Governor-General under the East India Company and became the first Viceroy when Queen Victoria's Proclamation transferred the governance of India directly to the British Crown.
Why others are wrong: Lord Dalhousie left India in 1856 before the revolt; Lord Lawrence served later from 1864 to 1869; Lord Mayo was Viceroy from 1869 until his assassination in 1872.

8. **The MacDonnell Committee, appointed during the viceroyalty of Lord Curzon, was established to address which recurring socio-economic crisis?**
   - A) Police Reforms
   - B) Currency and Banking regularizations
   - C) Famine Relief and Administration
   - D) Primary Education development
   - **Answer: C**
   - Explanation: Following the devastating famine of 1899â€“1900, Lord Curzon appointed the Famine Commission under Sir Antony MacDonnell in 1901 to suggest structural improvements in administering famine relief.
Why others are wrong: Police reforms were analyzed by the Fraser Commission; currency issues were reviewed by the Fowler Committee; education was reviewed by the Raleigh Commission.

9. **Which Viceroy of India was assassinated by a convict in the Andaman Islands in 1872 while on an official inspection tour?**
   - A) Lord Mayo
   - B) Lord Lytton
   - C) Lord Northbrook
   - D) Lord Dufferin
   - **Answer: A**
   - Explanation: Lord Mayo was stabbed to death by Sher Ali Afridi, a Pathan convict, at Port Blair in the Andaman Islands, making him the only Viceroy to be assassinated in office.
Why others are wrong: Lord Lytton resigned safely; Lord Northbrook resigned due to policy differences; Lord Dufferin oversaw the annexation of Upper Burma and left office routinely.

10. **The controversial Vernacular Press Act of 1878, which sought to gag Indian language newspapers criticizing British administrative policies, was enacted by whom?**
   - A) Lord Ripon
   - B) Lord Lytton
   - C) Lord Dufferin
   - D) Lord Lansdowne
   - **Answer: B**
   - Explanation: Lord Lytton passed the Vernacular Press Act to suppress the vocal criticism of British imperial policies found in vernacular newspapers, while exempting English-language publications.
Why others are wrong: Lord Ripon repealed this hated act in 1882; Lord Dufferin was Viceroy during the founding of the Indian National Congress; Lord Lansdowne dealt with factory reforms and borders.

11. **Who is widely remembered as the "Father of Local Self-Government" in India for introducing municipal and district board reforms in 1882?**
   - A) Lord Lytton
   - B) Lord Ripon
   - C) Lord Curzon
   - D) Lord Chelmsford
   - **Answer: B**
   - Explanation: Lord Ripon introduced a landmark resolution on local self-government in 1882, establishing local boards and municipal corporations to provide native Indians with administrative training and local autonomy.
Why others are wrong: Lord Lytton favored highly centralized bureaucratic control; Lord Curzon aggressively centralized power; Lord Chelmsford introduced dyarchy at the provincial level much later.

12. **The Ilbert Bill Controversy (1883), which exposed deep-seated racial arrogance among British residents in India, took place during whose administration?**
   - A) Lord Ripon
   - B) Lord Dufferin
   - C) Lord Elgin II
   - D) Lord Minto II
   - **Answer: A**
   - Explanation: The Ilbert Bill sought to allow senior Indian magistrates to try European offenders in criminal cases. It provoked an intense, racist backlash from Anglo-Indians, forcing Lord Ripon to compromise.
Why others are wrong: Lord Dufferin succeeded Ripon after the crisis ended; Lord Elgin II faced plague and localized tribal revolts; Lord Minto II oversaw separate electorates in 1909.

13. **The Hunter Commission of 1882 was appointed by the Government of India to investigate and recommend reforms in which specific area?**
   - A) Judicial corruption and delays
   - B) The state of education, particularly primary and secondary schooling
   - C) Land revenue relief policies during droughts
   - D) The recruitment process of the Indian Civil Service
   - **Answer: B**
   - Explanation: Appointed by Lord Ripon under the chairmanship of William Wilson Hunter, the Commission was tasked with reviewing the progress of education since the Wood's Despatch of 1854, heavily emphasizing local primary education.
Why others are wrong: Judicial processes were altered by internal legal codes; land revenue was addressed by separate regional revenue acts; Civil Service rules were reviewed by the Aitchison Commission in 1886.

14. **Which British Viceroy orchestrated the Partition of Bengal in 1905, citing administrative convenience but secretly aiming to weaken national unity?**
   - A) Lord Minto II
   - B) Lord Curzon
   - C) Lord Hardinge II
   - D) Lord Chelmsford
   - **Answer: B**
   - Explanation: Lord Curzon divided Bengal in 1905 into Western Bengal and Eastern Bengal & Assam, separating the Hindu-majority western regions from the Muslim-majority eastern regions to disrupt national movements.
Why others are wrong: Lord Minto II succeeded Curzon and handled the resulting Swadeshi movement; Lord Hardinge II annulled the partition in 1911; Lord Chelmsford co-authored the Montagu-Chelmsford reforms.

15. **The separate electorate system for Muslims, which institutionalized communal politics in India, was introduced through the reforms named after which Viceroy?**
   - A) Lord Minto II
   - B) Lord Morley
   - C) Lord Chelmsford
   - D) Lord Reading
   - **Answer: A**
   - Explanation: The Indian Councils Act 1909, known as the Morley-Minto Reforms, introduced separate electorates for Muslims. Lord Minto II is often called the 'Father of the Communal Electorate'.
Why others are wrong: Lord Morley was the Secretary of State, not the Viceroy; Lord Chelmsford was Viceroy during the 1919 Act; Lord Reading was Viceroy during the Non-Cooperation movement's later phase.

16. **The Sadler University Commission (1917â€“1919) was appointed to inquire into the systemic problems and academic failures of which specific university?**
   - A) Bombay University
